## 5. Strategic Options Considered

### Three lenses were built to compete, not to converge

The recommended strategy was not drafted by consensus. Three teams worked from the same 22-brief fact base and the same fact-check log, each charged with making the *strongest possible case* for a distinct strategic lens — and each required to quantify its initiatives, name its falsifiable metrics, publish an explicit "will not do" list, and state the best objection to its own thesis. A three-judge panel then scored the proposals independently on **capital efficiency** (growth dollars per invested dollar, risk-adjusted), **executability** (can this organization ship it on this timeline), and **competitive durability** (does the advantage survive a competent response). The recommendation in this document is the output of that contest: one proposal supplies the spine, and specific, named elements of the other two are grafted onto it where the judges found them superior.

| | **Option A: Fortress & Deepen** | **Option B: Channel & Digital Transformation** | **Option C: Expansion & Diversification** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Core bet | Win more of the fights already underway: recapture MA auto share, bundle to home parity, defend AAA, harvest MAIP credits | Become the most digitally capable agent carrier in MA, then bolt on a Prime-style bind-online channel | Cut the two concentration axes: product (home, small commercial) and state (NH/ME/VT, one bolt-on acquisition) |
| 2030–31 ambition | ~$270–375M identified premium + $20–40M/yr CR self-help; $450–600M+ swing vs. drift | Share ≥19.5% by YE2029; digital origination ≥4% of new business; ~$70–100M direct book by year 5 | +$500–650M new/defended DWP; MA personal auto from ~51% to ~40–43% of US book |
| Investment ask | ~$70–120M / 3 years | ~$70–105M / 5 years | ~$130–260M / 5 years (incl. $50–150M acquisition) |
| Signature exclusions | No new states, no greenfield direct, no M&A, no MAIP advocacy | No national DTC assault, no web rate on the agency entity, no core replatforming | No NY/NJ, no standalone workers' comp, no Verti relaunch |

Two-thirds of the content overlapped — all three teams independently converged on the AAA retention offensive, the Safeco transition desk, agent rater/API enablement, a symmetric MotionSmart refiling, the Plymouth Rock-pattern bind-online design, and claims-economics self-help. That convergence is itself a finding: these moves are robust to the choice of lens and appear in the recommended strategy unconditionally. The genuine disagreements — where the money goes at the margin, how fast direct scales, whether new states and acquisitions belong in the plan at all — are what the judges adjudicated, and they are the subject of this chapter.

### Option A — Fortress & Deepen: the arithmetic of home-market density won on all three panels

**The strongest case.** One point of Massachusetts private passenger auto share is ~$74M of annual premium on a $7.381B market [1] — winnable through ~1,300 agents already under contract, at rates already filed, on a Guidewire core live since 2020 [2]. The best expansion alternative anyone proposed (New Hampshire at a heroic 4% share) is worth ~$49M after a five-year, from-scratch campaign [3]. The homeowners-parity prize (~$275M of annual premium at auto-share parity) sits entirely with agents already appointed, in a line where MAPFRE ran a 30.0% loss ratio in 2024 [4]. The share loss that must be reversed — 22.1% to 18.33% of exposures, 2020 to Dec 2025, essentially all of it transferred to Progressive (5.1% → 12.17%) [5] — is a distribution-technology story, not a price-level story, and the margin repair that funds the response is complete (North America CR 105.4% → 95.4%, 2023→2025, record €139M result) [6]. Fortress also uniquely framed the ask the way Madrid decides: redeploy roughly one-third to one-half of the $246.7M of dividends Commerce exported through its loss-and-recovery years (2019–23) [7], hold the CR at a ~95–96% U.S. bar, zero new-state capital, zero M&A.

**The strongest objection, taken seriously.** Fortress Massachusetts risks polishing a melting ice cube: a flat-exposure state, an aging population, an AM Best standalone assessment of "marginal operating performance" that leans on Madrid [8], and a plan with no Plan B if Progressive — structurally advantaged in brand, telematics data, and dual-channel economics — simply keeps winning. Spending ~$100M to deepen the very concentration rating agencies penalize is not obviously prudent.

**The judges' conclusion.** Fortress scored highest on all three panels — capital efficiency 86, executability 84, competitive durability 84 — and its answer to the concentration objection was decisive: MAPFRE's fragility is not that it is *in* Massachusetts (structurally one of the better auto states: 62.2% statewide loss ratio in 2024 [9]; rating-factor bans that neutralize national carriers' segmentation weapons [10]); it is that it is *monoline auto with a shallow moat inside the state* — under-bundled, under-telematic, claims-weak, dependent on one affinity partner. The Fortress initiatives attack exactly those axes, which is what improves the standalone AM Best profile; a subscale second state does not. The panel also credited two disciplines the other proposals lacked in places: defended premium never counted as growth, and every initiative carrying a dated, falsifiable tripwire. Its weaknesses — a thinner treatment of commercial lines and no answer on the strategically orphaned West Coast block — were repairable by graft.

### Option B — Channel & Digital Transformation: right diagnosis, one disqualifying omission, one killed forecast

**The strongest case.** Transform's diagnosis is the sharpest single sentence in the engagement: MAPFRE is not being beaten on price, product, or capital — it is being beaten on distribution technology, by a competitor that attacks through MAPFRE's own agents *and* through a direct channel MAPFRE does not have. The evidence is uncomfortable and specific: real-time in-AMS appetite is now agents' #1 carrier-selection factor (29%, up from 12%), 83% of agents say they would write more with it, and MAPFRE is absent from EZLynx's Massachusetts panel — the rater stack national consolidators standardize on [11][12]. Meanwhile the quote-but-no-bind website converts MAPFRE brand demand into Progressive and GEICO policies during record shopping (47.1% of policies shopped in 12 months) and a minimum-limits repricing wave [13]. Transform's channel-conflict engineering was the best in the engagement: bind online at existing filed rates, every policy assigned to a local agent for a one-time fee with full commission and lifetime ownership (the Plymouth Rock mechanic, running in Massachusetts since October 2015 [14]); owned traffic first; aggregator buys capped at calibration size; scale only where measured CAC beats the $500–900/bound-policy benchmark [14]; the Citation/American Commerce paired-entity filing held in reserve. Its claims diagnosis — that the constraint is operating model, not software, in an organization that fielded ePICS, One Inc, and Shift without moving a six-year bottom-tier J.D. Power record (detailed in the Situation Assessment) [15] — survives intact into Pillar 4.

**The strongest objection, taken seriously.** Direct is a decade-long money pit and a channel-conflict grenade: Plymouth Rock's own Core Direct reached only ~$110M after ten years [14], Progressive's direct channel runs 4.6 CR points worse than its agency channel even at scale (89.6 vs. 85.0, YTD Sept 2025) [16], and every dollar spent on bind-online is a dollar not spent on the agent war MAPFRE is actually losing.

**The judges' conclusion.** Two findings ended Transform's candidacy as the spine. First, the omission of homeowners was disqualifying on capital efficiency: a channel strategy that ignores the single cheapest growth pool in the franchise (~$275M parity gap, 30.0% loss ratio, 95%-vs-85% bundled retention [4][17]) optimizes the pipe while leaving the highest-margin product off the shelf — and forfeits the retention armor that makes recaptured share durable. Second, **all three judges killed the year-5 direct scale forecast** (40–55K policies, $70–100M): it extrapolated past Plymouth Rock's decade of documented experience on the strength of brand-share assumptions no MA precedent supports. What survives is the pilot (10K policies, ~$17–20M premium, $5–9M spend, partially offset by agent fees) and a 15–25K steady state — with measured CAC and channel loss ratio, not the deck, authorizing anything more. The durable verdict on Transform: the best *how* in the engagement, attached to an incomplete *what*.

### Option C — Expansion & Diversification: the correct anxiety, the wrong prescription — and three grafts the spine needed

**The strongest case.** Expand made the concentration argument with rigor the other teams lacked, correcting the client's own framing: ~75–80% of US premium is Massachusetts *all-lines*, but MA personal auto alone is ~51% — two axes, requiring different treatments [18]. Its failure file is real (Merced insolvent within a month of the Camp Fire; twelve Louisiana insurers, 2021–23; Fireman's Fund's renewal rights sold at ~26 cents per premium dollar [19]), and its warning is legitimate: AM Best explicitly subsidizes the US rating with Madrid's diversification [8]. Its program was disciplined by expansion standards — contiguous states only, no new licenses, distribution-led, one "complementary single-state" acquisition squarely inside Madrid's stated M&A frame, with kill gates throughout. And it was the only team that caught two board-level facts: the **CAR commercial servicing-carrier appointment** (one of four seats, five-year term expiring 12/31/2026, RFP imminent) [20], and the ~4.5% YoY shrinkage of Commerce's #1 MA commercial-auto line while the market grew ~3% and Arbella grew ~14% [20].

**The strongest objection, taken seriously.** This is the same company that expanded, failed, and amputated (2017–2022: five state exits, Florida runoff, Verti USA's silent death), told investors in Feb 2020 it wanted *more* exits — a record every recruited agent can discover — and whose parent said in February 2026 it is "not planning to broaden our footprint" [21]. Diversification for a carrier without a demonstrated operating edge is diversified mediocrity.

**The judges' conclusion.** The expansion core failed on capital efficiency and executability. The entire New Hampshire prize at a heroic 4% share (~$49M) is two-thirds of one Massachusetts share point winnable at marginal cost — fought against Safety and Plymouth Rock on shelves they own, with guaranteed profit-sharing floors the panel scored as an unpriced contingent liability [3]. The $50–300M bolt-on was priced off a distressed 2015 comp no 2026 seller will honor, was silent on ~$100M+ of supporting surplus (true ask $150–250M), and ran 2–3x Madrid's demonstrated ~$95M bolt-on appetite [21] — buying premium is not growth, and the panel refused to book it. The ACMIC-template equity JV pitch split the panel — two judges killed it, one grafted it — and the kill was honored on the evidence: the live precedent required a Q4-2025 capital call and spent months under AM Best review with negative implications before its December 2025 affirmation; conceding equity is the tax currently bleeding MAPFRE, not a strategy [8]. But three Expand elements scored above their Fortress and Transform equivalents and were grafted whole: the CAR reappointment bid, the 90-day commercial-auto leak diagnosis before any commercial investment, and the West Coast hold/fix/sell review — the last converting a strategically orphaned CA/OR/AZ block into a capital-release option that could offset a substantial share of the Massachusetts program's cost (the block's premium and allocated surplus are a data-room item).

### The verdict, and what the recommended strategy takes from each

The panel's synthesis logic is worth stating plainly, because it is the answer to the question a skeptical reader should ask: *if Fortress won, why does the final strategy contain a direct channel, a commercial-lines workstream, and a divestment review?*

**Fortress supplies the spine** — the thesis (density beats every alternative by an order of magnitude), the Madrid framing (dividend redeployment, CR guardrail, zero new states, zero M&A), the arithmetic discipline (defended ≠ growth; every initiative dated and falsifiable), and the earn-the-right gate: only if YE2028 lands on the modeled trajectory (auto ≥18.6% and rising, home ≥11.7% with attach ≥40%, CR ≤96% — checkpoints on the path to ≥19% auto and ~13% home by 2030–31) does the diversification conversation reopen — at which point a bind-capable platform, repaired claims economics, and a scaled telematics program make any later adjacency dramatically cheaper. Deepening first is what makes expanding later affordable.

**Transform supplies the how**: the bind-online design with its channel-conflict guardrails kept verbatim, the sequencing rule (agent enablement visibly first, bind later), the CAC gates, the paired-entity reserve, the falsifiable-metrics governance scaffold, and the claims operating-model fix — claims-experience KPIs on the same executive scorecard as the combined ratio.

**Expand supplies the corrections**: the CAR seat (one of only two hard 12/31/2026 deadlines in the plan), the commercial-auto diagnosis, the West Coast review, and the two-axis concentration decomposition used in the Financial Business Case. Its deeper contribution is negative and permanent: by making the best possible case for expansion and still losing on the arithmetic, it converted "no new states this cycle" from an instinct into a tested conclusion.

Where the judges split, the resolutions are recorded in the plan as standing constraints: club partnerships survive only in non-equity form; the home ambition holds at Fortress's +2.5 points (to ~13%) with full parity named as post-2030 upside; the urban/trilingual program is pilot-gated per the executability judge (2–3 territories, ~3,000 voluntary car years by mid-2028, budget raised to $10–18M for the genuine bilingual-talent build); the coastal tranche takes the stricter $30–40M cap.

### The road not taken — killed on the merits, kept killed by design

The table below records the options where the judges genuinely debated before killing — with the deciding fact in each case. The full standing-constraint register (ten items, board-resolution language) appears in The Recommended Strategy under "What MAPFRE will NOT do"; it exists precisely so that killed initiatives cannot re-enter through workstreams, because the panel judged management bandwidth, not capital, to be the program's scarcest resource.

| Rejected option | Proposed by | Deciding fact |
|---|---|---|
| NH/ME/VT agency build-outs; CT fix-then-grow | Expand | Whole NH prize at 4% ≈ $49M — two-thirds of one MA point at marginal cost, against incumbents who own the shelves [1][3]; profit-sharing floors an unpriced contingent liability |
| $50–300M single-state bolt-on | Expand | Priced off Fireman's Fund's distressed 26¢ comp [19]; true capital ask $150–250M vs. Madrid's ~$95M demonstrated appetite [21]; demoted to unfunded opportunistic screen |
| Equity/ACMIC-template club JV | Expand (split panel, 2–1 kill) | Live precedent required a Q4-2025 capital call and months under AM Best review with negative implications before its Dec 2025 affirmation [8]; non-equity second-carrier slot survives as settlement architecture only |
| Year-5 direct scale case ($70–100M) | Transform | Killed by all three judges; 10x Plymouth Rock's documented decade [14]; pilot + 15–25K steady state funded, CAC-gated tranches thereafter |
| Inorganic BOP/CMP acceleration ($20–40M) | Expand | Scale/STP arms race: carriers >$2.5B took 12 share points in six years; Safety needed two decades to reach ~$32M of BOP [20] |

The remaining kills — NY/NJ re-entry, a greenfield direct brand or Verti relaunch, standalone workers' comp, un-gated commission escalation, scale aggregator buying, further MAIP credit advocacy, and West Coast growth capital or drift — were screened out by all three teams or self-rejected by their proposers; each appears with its deciding fact in the standing-constraint register [14][20][21][22][23].

The pattern across the kill list is consistent: every rejected option either bought growth at a multiple of the cost of the next Massachusetts dollar, or bet the program's scarcest resource — executive attention in an organization that has shipped no growth-side or agent-facing capability on its modern core in six years — on fights outside MAPFRE's structural advantages. The recommended strategy spends both dollars and attention where the arithmetic, the judges, and MAPFRE's own history all point: at home.

## 6. The Recommended Strategy

### The thesis: win more of the fights MAPFRE is already in — and do it inside a window that closes

**MAPFRE's growth strategy is to convert an inertia-held #1 Massachusetts franchise into a defended, densified, digitally-armed one — during a 12–24 month window in which every major competitor is disrupted, repricing, or distracted, and after which the window does not reopen.** No new states. No M&A. No greenfield brands. The entire program is executed through assets already owned: ~1,300 appointed Massachusetts agencies [18], 908,833 in-force auto exposures [1], the state's largest group-discount franchise [19], a #1 homeowners position at half its natural size [22], and a modern Guidewire core live since 2020 with no growth-side or agent-facing capability yet built on top of it [51].

The thesis rests on four facts, each verified in the fact-check log:

**1. The arithmetic of home-market density beats every alternative by an order of magnitude.** One point of Massachusetts private passenger auto share is ~$74M of annual premium on a $7.381B market [2] — winnable through agents already under contract, at rates already filed, on a platform already built. The best expansion alternative anyone proposed — New Hampshire at a heroic 4% share of its $1.22B market [2] — is worth ~$49M after a five-year, from-scratch campaign against Safety and Plymouth Rock on shelves they own. The full homeowners-parity prize (~$275M of annual premium at auto-share parity [22][2]) sits with agents already appointed. There is no growth dollar available anywhere in the Northeast as cheap as the next dollar in Massachusetts.

**2. The margin repair is complete — and it was paid for in share.** North America went from a 105.4% combined ratio in 2023 [4] to 95.4% in 2025, a record €139M result with U.S. auto at 97.8% [3], bought with >29% of cumulative rate [4] and roughly four points of Massachusetts auto share (22.1% → 18.33%), essentially all of it transferred to Progressive (5.1% → 12.17%) — much of it through MAPFRE's own agents [1]. The share loss is a distribution-technology and pricing-tools story, not a price-level story. For the first time in a decade MAPFRE has both the underwriting headroom and Madrid's explicit profitable-growth mandate to buy the share back.

**3. The window is real, dated, and does not reopen.** The four hard clocks documented in the Situation Assessment all expire inside the plan's first 18 months: the >$200M AAA agreement (12/31/2026, migration concentrating in January 2027+ renewals [6][7][8]); the CAR commercial servicing seat (12/31/2026, RFP imminent [28][29]); the Safeco recruiting window (open now, uncontested as of July 2026, realistically 6–12 months [10][11]); and competitors' repricing cycle (Safety, Plymouth Rock and Arbella filing increases into late 2026 while Commerce at a 97.8% auto CR [3] can hold rate). The window governs when the *positions* are cheap — the book, the agencies, the seat, the filings; the premium those positions produce compounds through 2028–31.

**4. MAPFRE's right to win rests on assets competitors cannot copy quickly or cheaply.** Progressive has no Massachusetts homeowners machine at scale, zero §193R group deviations against Commerce's 280 — the largest franchise in the state, with the participation rule waived through 2030 [19][20][21] — and no case for building Portuguese-language claims operations for one state's urban credit territories. The deviation advantage is scale, incumbency and first-mover position, not statutory exclusion: §193R filings are open to any carrier, and the honest framing is a 12–24 month replication lag (group-by-group sponsor negotiation and filing) against an incumbent holding the anchor relationships — the plan's answer to "what if Progressive files deviations in 2027?" is set out under Initiative 2b. GEICO's only weapon — price plus $1.4B of national media — touches none of the agent-side battlefield in the most IA-penetrated state in America (78.8% of P&C premium [53]). Safety and Plymouth Rock can match individual moves, but at proportionally greater cost against a carrier with double their scale and the market's largest cross-sell base. And Massachusetts' rating-factor bans (211 CMR 79.04, enumerated in the Market & Competitive Landscape) neutralize the national carriers' biggest segmentation weapons: the rules reward the local incumbent that executes.

**How the strategy compounds.** Each pillar arms the next. Winning the agent's desktop (Pillar 1) is the delivery rail for the AAA re-anchoring and the bundle offensive (Pillar 2); the bundle is the retention armor that makes recaptured share durable; telematics and the urban credit harvest (Pillar 3) are the only pricing and customer-pool levers Massachusetts law permits, and they run on the bind-capable digital rail; claims self-help (Pillar 4) funds the program's opex and repairs the one customer-facing weakness that makes an inertia-held #1 share unstable. By YE2028, if the trajectory gates are met — **MA auto ≥18.6% and rising for two consecutive quarters; MA home ≥11.7% with bundle attach ≥40%; combined ratio ≤96%**, checkpoints set on the modeled path to ≥19% auto and ~13% home by 2030–31 — MAPFRE will have *earned* the right to reopen the diversification conversation, with a bind-capable platform, repaired claims economics, and a telematics program that make any later adjacency dramatically cheaper. The fortress is not the alternative to expansion; it is the precondition for it.

**The ask to Madrid is framed the only way Madrid decides:** $85–135M over three years (~1–1.5% of premium per year), sequenced with ~$45–60M in year 1 — roughly one-third to one-half of the $246.7M of dividends Commerce exported to the parent through its loss-and-recovery years of 2019–23 [5]. The recommended mechanism is a **two-to-three-year reduced-dividend agreement** — retained U.S. earnings rather than a capital injection, a materially easier conversation given Commerce's $561–684M surplus and ~€118M annual U.S. result. The program is substantially self-funded by $20–40M per year of claims-economics self-help (Pillar 4) and held throughout at a ~95–96% U.S. combined-ratio bar — a planning assumption to be ratified in the funding paper, since the U.S. book runs above the group's stated 93–94% 2026 target on Massachusetts mix — with zero new-state capital and zero M&A.

Every initiative below carries an owner, a budget, a horizon, its five-year premium arithmetic, and a falsifiable leading indicator. Two accounting disciplines apply throughout: impact figures are annual premium by 2030–31 at risk-adjusted midpoints unless stated, and **"defended" premium is counted separately from growth and never double-counted**.

---

### Pillar 1 — Win the agent's desktop: shelf position, ease of doing business, and the two perishable windows

*Owner: Chief Distribution Officer. Budget: $28–42M over three years. This pillar is first, largest, and unconditional — it arms everything else.*

**1a. Rater ubiquity and straight-through processing (0–12 months).** Ship real-time rating and appetite APIs from the existing Guidewire stack [51] into every rater Massachusetts agents and their acquirers use: harden SinglePoint and Vertafore PL Rating, **close the EZLynx gap** — MAPFRE is absent from EZLynx's Massachusetts panel [16], the stack national consolidators standardize on — plus flawless IVANS download, STP new-business issuance for clean risks, and agent-facing digital claims transparency. *Why it wins:* real-time appetite is now agents' #1 carrier-selection factor (29%, up from 12% a year earlier) [13]; 83% of agents say they would write more with real-time quoting in their AMS [14]; only 40% of personal-lines agents call their carriers easy to work with, and the satisfaction gap for "very easy" carriers is 274 points [15]. This layer is table stakes Progressive has already set — absence is fatal even though presence alone is not advantage. *Risk:* enablement achieves parity, not superiority; the metric below is the tripwire that triggers re-allocation, not escalation. *Metric: share of top-400-agency new-business quotes that include a MAPFRE rate, measured quarterly; live on the EZLynx MA panel by Q2 2027.*

**1b. Consolidator platform agreements (0–18 months).** Pre-negotiated, change-of-control-surviving platform agreements with the 10–15 acquirers rolling up MAPFRE's plant — Kaplansky/Alera, Duffy, DiVirgilio, World, Trucordia, Patriot Growth, King Risk, Hilb [17] — with Ivans Bookroll tooling and dedicated service teams. *Why it wins:* ~2.3% of MAPFRE's ~1,300 Massachusetts agencies change ownership annually (31 deals in 2025) [17][18] — roughly 12% of the plant over the plan horizon, an estimated **$150–160M of premium at risk** (planning estimate on book-size averages). Acquired agencies migrate to their platform's standard rater stack and compensation agreements; a carrier without a platform deal inherits the runoff. The ownership layer is the one place Progressive cannot follow. *Risk:* platform deals concentrate negotiating power; mitigated by loss-ratio-gated economics (1d).

**1c. The Safeco transition desk — launched this quarter.** Fast-track appointments, a Bookroll-equipped book-roll team, and the one pledge Liberty cannot make and MAPFRE credibly can *today*: "we will never out-advertise you for your own customer." *Why it wins:* the pool is ~22,000 ex-Safeco agencies nationally [10]; Liberty holds ~247K Massachusetts auto exposures (4.98% and shrinking) [1] and cut U.S. Retail written premium 6.4% in 2025 [12]; documented agent sentiment mixes brand grief with distrust of Liberty's own direct channel. As of July 2026 no competitor has publicly launched a recruiting campaign — the window is open and uncontested. **This is the one initiative where a 90-day delay destroys the thesis**: the realistic uncontested window is 6–12 months before Safety or Plymouth Rock stand up their own desks. *Risk:* rolled books adversely select; underwrite book rolls at file level, not agency level. *Metric: ex-Safeco agency appointments and rolled exposures, reported monthly to the CEO.*

**1d. Loss-ratio-gated compensation redesign (12–24 months).** Tiered agent clubs on the Safety architecture — but strictly loss-ratio-gated: growth and retention kickers pay only where agency-level economics improve with the book. An un-gated commission escalation is the one move Safety matches within a single cycle at symmetric cost, leaving both carriers poorer. We will not start that arms race (see §"What MAPFRE will not do," item 7).

**Pillar 1 arithmetic.** Defense: ~$150–160M of consolidation-exposed premium protected over the plan. Offense: +1.0 point of MA auto share recaptured by 2029 from Safety/Plymouth Rock/Arbella repricing slippage, Safeco disruption, and GEICO defectors ≈ **$74M**; a 10% capture of the Safeco pool ≈ 25K exposures ≈ **$35M**. Share-capture rates are planning estimates; the quarterly quote-stack metric is the falsifiable tripwire.

---

### Pillar 2 — Defend and densify the book: the AAA endgame, the group machine, the bundle fortress, and the commercial-auto seat

*Owners: Chief Marketing/Affinity Officer (2a, 2b); Chief Product Officer, Personal Lines (2c); President of Commercial Lines (2d). Budget: $38–61M over three years plus priced discount costs. This pillar carries the two hardest deadlines in the plan — both December 31, 2026.*

**2a. The AAA retention offensive — run in 2026, before the January 2027 renewal wall.** Plan as if the JMA is gone; the injunction denial makes that the base case [7]. Three moves. (i) A retention task force on the ~$200M AAA-sourced book [6] (~100K+ policies; the AAA-sourced policy file is data-room request #1): agent-of-record re-anchoring into the regular agency channel, bundle attach at the filed account discount, continuity pricing within the filed structure. (ii) Repurpose the litigation as settlement leverage for the *book*, not the brand: renewal-rights protections, member-data and non-solicitation terms, or a paid second-carrier slot inside AAA's multi-carrier agency — MCIC's 370-job Rhode Island servicing build-out signals a three-to-four-year migration plan [9], which is time MAPFRE can use. (iii) Honest triage: retention targets are premium-quality-weighted, not policy-count-weighted — if MCIC wants to buy the underpriced bottom quartile with ACE-pool subsidy, let it. *Arithmetic:* passive runoff retains ~25–30% (≈ −$140–150M by 2029); the managed target is 50–60% retention. The delta versus passive is 20–35 retention points on a >$200M book — **$40–70M/yr of defended premium** — the largest defended-dollars-per-dollar-spent item on the board, at $10–20M of cost. *Risk:* MCIC prices irrationally low with pool subsidy; the bundle, not price, is the counter — a home+auto+umbrella account is roughly three times harder to move. *Metric: monthly CAR share in AAA-heavy territories; AAA-book retention through the January 2027 wall; re-scope gate Q2 2027.*

**2b. Industrialize the group-deviation machine.** Deepen the top 30–50 anchor groups from Commerce's 4% modal discount to 5–6% (peers' modal deviation is 5%, and approved maxima run to 9–10% [19][20]); add payroll deduction and co-branded digital enrollment — on the Pillar 3 bind rail — for the credit unions and banks already on Commerce's approved list (DCU, Eastern Bank, et al.). Cost ~$3–6M/yr in rate. *Why it wins:* this is the regulator-sanctioned headline price weapon that Progressive (zero deviations) and GEICO (eight) structurally cannot match [20], with the 35% participation rule waived through 2030 [21]. The realistic AAA replacement is this *portfolio*, not a single new partner — no Massachusetts group approaches AAA's 1.2M member households. *Arithmetic: 10–20K new group policies over three years ≈ **$18–36M**.* *Risk:* deviation deepening leaks margin if un-targeted; deepen only anchor groups with measured loss ratios. *The honest stress test — what if Progressive files deviations in 2027?* §193R is open to any carrier; Progressive's zero is a choice, reversible with routine DOI filings. What protects the franchise is position, not statute: 280 groups against Plymouth Rock's 117 (5% modal, 10% max), Safety's 68, Arbella's 72 and GEICO's 8 [20]; the anchor sponsor relationships (no Massachusetts group approaches AAA's 1.2M member households, and the largest sponsors are already on Commerce's approved list); and the 12–24 months any entrant needs to negotiate sponsors and file group by group. A Progressive entry in 2027 starts a franchise build MAPFRE completed years ago — and this initiative widens the gap it would have to close. Note also that deepening to 5–6% reaches Plymouth Rock's existing 5% modal: the durable advantage is the breadth of the 280-group portfolio and its anchors, not the headline percentage — the quarterly deviation-ladder metric tracks both.

**2c. Homeowners to ~13%: the bundle offensive, with a hard-gated coastal tranche.** The closest thing to free growth in the stack. MAPFRE is #1 in Massachusetts home at 10.62% — barely half its auto share — in a line that ran a 30.0% loss ratio in 2024 [22], in a market that grew 12.7% while MAPFRE's voluntary share slid from 12.5% to 11.0% [23], with property-cat reinsurance 10–12% cheaper at the January 2026 renewal [26] and the full parity gap worth ~$275M through agents already under contract [22][2]. Mechanics: measure bundle attach on the 909K-exposure auto book (data-room request #2); agency-level attach targets with attach compensation; pre-filled cross-sell at every auto new business; renters as the feeder (bundled renters retain at 95% vs. 82% [24]); umbrella *only* as conservatively underwritten bundle cement over MAPFRE auto+home — industry personal umbrella ran a ~200% combined ratio in 2024 [25] and is never a standalone line here. Coastal: a wind-managed tranche — inspection-driven, bundled-only, percentage wind deductibles, **capped at $30–40M DWP** — pre-positioned now but **gated on an actual MPIUA (FAIR Plan) rate filing**: the moratorium expired March 2026, no filing has appeared, and the hearing process gives 12+ months of lead time [23]. *Arithmetic:* home share 10.6% → ~13.1% by 2030; the impact is the incremental share only, valued at the model's conservative ~$4.3B 2030 market — (13.1% − 10.62%) × ~$4.3B ≈ $107M, risk-adjusted to **$95–115M** of annual premium (market growth on the base share is excluded as upside, per the Financial Business Case's discipline #1) — with full parity (~$275M) named as post-2030 upside. Bundle retention spillover: lifting attach from an assumed ~25% to 40% moves ~136K customers from 85% to 95% retention [24] → ~13,600 fewer defections/yr × ~$1,450 average premium ≈ **~$20M/yr of auto premium retained, compounding — counted as defended premium, never as growth**. Mix effect: every home dollar written at these loss ratios is combined-ratio-accretive. *Risk:* the 30% loss-ratio year was benign weather, not skill; an immature coastal book plus one Northeast hurricane is a ratings event on a $561–684M surplus base [5] — hence the cap, the gate, and the inland/suburban core. *Metric: bundle attach rate quarterly; home share semi-annually.*

**2d. Commercial auto: fix the leak, win the CAR seat.** Two moves only. (i) A **90-day diagnosis** of why Commerce's #1 Massachusetts commercial-auto line shrank ~4.5% year-over-year into August 2025 while the market grew ~3% and Arbella grew ~14% [27] — deliberate discipline or share loss — before any commercial investment decision. (ii) An all-out bid to **retain the CAR commercial servicing-carrier appointment**: one of four seats, five-year term ending December 31, 2026, RFP imminent [28][29]. The seat confers expense-allowance income, agent stickiness in hard-to-place classes, and an information moat over a ceded pool of ~$260M — roughly 18–19% of the state's commercial-auto market — at near-zero capital [28]. On the board agenda this month. *Arithmetic: reversing the −4.5% leak and regaining ~1.5–2.5 points of the $1.43B commercial-auto market ≈ **+$25–35M** by 2031 (share-driven, valued at the 2024 market per the Financial Business Case's discipline #1; market growth on the held book is unbooked upside), at commercial-lines margins (MAPFRE's regional non-auto P&C ran a 78.9% combined ratio in 2025 [3] — mix-accretive).* No BOP/CMP acquisition spend this cycle: Safety needed two decades to build a $32M BOP book [37] (see "What MAPFRE will not do," item 4). *Risk:* losing the seat to a hungrier bidder; the RFP war room stands up in week one.

---

### Pillar 3 — Price and reach where competitors can't: telematics, the urban credit harvest, and the agent-assigned digital rail

*Owners: Chief Actuary/Pricing (3a); GM, New Segments (3b); Chief Digital Officer (3c). Budget: $19–35M over three years. Sequencing rule: 3c launches only after Pillar 1's agent investments are visible to agents.*

**3a. Re-arm MotionSmart — one filing, symmetric by design.** Refile from today's token "up to 2%" [31] — down from up to 10% at enrollment and up to 20% at renewal at the July 2021 launch [32] — to an **8–10% at-quote participation discount with a symmetric ±20% renewal swing**, filed as one structure from day one. This sits squarely inside revealed DOI tolerance: Plymouth Rock's DriveSavely holds 10% at quote and a −20%/+20% renewal swing [30], and Snapshot operates in Massachusetts with surcharges. CMT — already MAPFRE's vendor and headquartered in its home market — is the delivery partner; the program is built privacy-native (opt-in, first-party, no third-party sharing) for either version of the pending Massachusetts Data Privacy Act. Fast-follow filing: a third-party at-quote driving score (Arity IQ / TransUnion-CMT) — no approved Massachusetts filing uses one, the sole first-mover element in this plan; file first and expect Progressive to follow within 12 months. **Kill trigger built in: if the DOI approves discount-only, do not launch.** That is MotionSmart 1.0 again — discount-only donates premium on good risks; Progressive's UBI economics only turned decisively positive after surcharge capability arrived in 2014 [33]. *Why it wins:* under Massachusetts' rating-factor bans (enumerated in the Market & Competitive Landscape), telematics is the only high-dimensional pricing lever the law permits, and the carrier taking MAPFRE's share calls UBI its single most predictive variable at 3x the next best [33]. This stops Snapshot's skim of MAPFRE's best risks. *Arithmetic:* 30% new-business enrollment by 2028 (Allstate's 30–40% penetration in offered states is the proven benchmark [34]) builds an enrolled book worth **0.5–1.0 portfolio combined-ratio points by year 4** (≈$12–25M/yr of loss-cost advantage on the Massachusetts book), plus documented retention lift — Progressive's top discount earners renew at rates six points higher [33]. Investment <$10M. *Metric: filing approved as symmetric by mid-2027; new-business enrollment rate quarterly.*

**3b. Harvest the MAIP credit system: the trilingual urban franchise — pilot first.** Stop the regulatory crusade — MAPFRE has backed credit phase-outs and lost every time since 2016, and the 2026 CAR Actuarial Committee again protected the DOI-territory credits [35]; arbitrage beats advocacy. Harvest instead the ~$1.36B of credit premium used annually to offset MAIP assignments, 73.8% of it generated in 11 urban territories at ~$2,900 of average offset per voluntary car year [35]. The double-paid economics: Commerce absorbs ~1.7x its pro-rata MAIP share (30.4% of MAIP assignments against an 18.1% voluntary share [36]; Safety runs ~0.7x [37]), worth an estimated $3–9M/yr of underwriting drag (assumption-flagged; calibrated to CAR exhibit averages). Every voluntary urban car year written in the credit territories earns adequately priced new premium *and* ~$2,900 of quota-share offset [35] — and those territories are where Massachusetts actually grows: Latinos were roughly 8 in 10 net new residents from 2014–23 [48], Brazilians are the state's largest immigrant group at ~140K+, Portuguese-speaking and owned by no carrier [49], and MAPFRE's Puerto Rico operation is a genuine brand bridge into Springfield, Holyoke and Boston. Build: bilingual Latino- and Brazilian-owned agency recruitment; end-to-end Spanish **and Portuguese** sales, service and claims — translation alone fails (~40% of Spanish-dominant consumers only make high-value purchases with native-language support [50]); Rule 29.F credit purchases as a bridge tactic. **Pilot first: the 2–3 richest credit-factor territories, targeting ~3,000 voluntary car years by mid-2028; the 10K-car commitment and the full trilingual claims build are gated on pilot loss ratios and service metrics.** The talent lift — bilingual adjusters and CSRs — is real and was underpriced in earlier drafts; budget $10–18M. Telematics-at-quote (3a) is the urban underwriting tool. *Full-scale arithmetic: +10,000 voluntary urban car years ≈ **$20–29M premium**, plus ~$29M of credit premium cutting assignment intake 25–35% and shedding **$3–9M/yr of drag** [35][36]. Timing: MAIP rates are the most adequate in years (assignments −38.4% in 2025 after the July 2025 filing [35]) — the risk of writing urban voluntary business is at its cyclical low.* *Risk:* pilot loss ratios underperform; mitigants are Commerce's own adequate rates (the statutory "lower-of" rule bites least now), telematics-at-quote underwriting, and starting in the 1.25–1.75x credit-factor cells where offset economics are richest.

**3c. Bind-online with agent assignment — the Prime clone, at pilot scale, sequenced last.** Enable quote-to-bind on the existing website at existing filed rates, with every policy assigned to a local agent for a one-time marketing fee, full commission, and lifetime ownership of the account — the Plymouth Rock eSales mechanic, proven in Massachusetts continuously since October 2015 [39]. A $5–15M MVP on the already-built Guidewire core [51], launched with agent advisory council endorsement **only after Pillar 1 deliverables are visible to agents**; abandoned and completed quotes recycled to agents as consented leads — **and this lead-routing mechanic launches immediately, in the first 100 days, as a zero-conflict interim fix for the funnel leak while the bind rail waits its turn in the sequence**; the Citation/American Commerce paired-entity filing — the accepted Massachusetts template for channel separation since Progressive's 2008 dual-entity structure — held in reserve, unused unless channel separation is ever needed. Guardrails, verbatim: owned traffic first; aggregator buys capped at calibration size with loss-ratio gates (Plymouth Rock's ~$260M aggregator-sourced Partner book ran elevated loss ratios for years [38]); scale paid digital only where measured CAC beats the $500–900 per-bound-policy benchmark [40]. **The year-5 scale forecast is killed: budget the 10K-policy pilot (~$17–20M premium, $5–9M acquisition spend, partially offset by agent fees) and a 15–25K-policy steady state (~$25–40M). Measured CAC and channel loss ratio — not the deck — authorize anything more.** *Why it wins even at modest premium:* strategic value exceeds premium value. This is the delivery rail for AAA re-anchoring offers, group digital enrollment, in-language purchase, and telematics-at-quote — and it closes the self-inflicted leak of a quote page that today hands MAPFRE brand demand to Progressive's funnel during record shopping (47.1% of in-force policies shopped [46]) and the minimum-limits repricing wave (+36% on minimum-coverage premiums since July 2025 [47]). Competitors cannot compete away the closing of your own funnel. *Risk:* channel conflict; the agent-assignment mechanic, sequencing rule, and advisory-council launch are the mitigation — Plymouth Rock has operated this promise for a decade without losing its agency franchise [38][39].

---

### Pillar 4 — Self-fund, repair the flank, and release capital: the claims engine, the experience turnaround, and the West Coast review

*Owners: Chief Claims Officer (4a, 4b); CFO (4c). Budget: $12–22M over three years, with 12–24 month payback. This pillar is what lets the CFO tell Madrid the program is substantially self-funded.*

**4a. Claims-economics self-help.** Subrogation analytics first: the industry recovers on average 4.5% of net claims paid through salvage and subrogation (6.2% among firms with any recovery) [41], roughly 15% of claims close with a missed subrogation opportunity, and industry leakage runs $15–20B annually [42] — recovery-detection models plus a dedicated unit are the fastest-payback claims investment available. Then scale what is already licensed but under-deployed: ePICS photo appraisal pushed toward touchless norms, two-way claims texting, One Inc instant payment as the default, and the DRP repositioned for the ADAS and labor-rate era — calibrations now appear on 23–35% of appraisals [54], and MAPFRE holds one of three insurer seats on the state's labor-rate Advisory Board. *Arithmetic:* U.S. paid losses ≈ $2.5B premium × ~65% loss ratio ≈ $1.6B; +0.5–1.0 point of net recovery = **$8–16M/yr, rate-free**; touchless penetration and steering a further 0.3–0.5 CR points (**$7–12M/yr**). Combined: **$20–40M/yr ≈ 1–1.5 combined-ratio points of self-help** funding the program's opex. *Risk:* tool deployment without operating-model change — which is exactly the pattern to date, hence 4b.

**4b. The operating-model fix — non-negotiable.** This organization has already fielded ePICS, One Inc, and Shift without moving its six-year bottom-tier J.D. Power record (detailed in the Situation Assessment) [44][45]. The constraint is operating model, not software. The fix: claims-experience KPIs — touchless share, cycle time, texted-claims share, claimant NPS — go on the same executive scorecard as the combined ratio, managed to cycle-time and NPS targets, never headcount targets. Repair cycle time is the industry's proven retention lever (the 2025 industry improvement to 19.3 days from 22.3 drove the year's satisfaction gain [43]) — and claimants are the book's most shop-prone customers.

**4c. West Coast hold/fix/sell review — year 1.** The California/Oregon/Arizona block (Commerce West) is strategically orphaned from the New England franchise: it shares no distribution, no regulatory regime, and no brand density with the business this strategy defends. A disciplined review in 2026–27 creates a capital-release option that could offset a substantial share of the Massachusetts program's cost — the block's premium and allocated surplus are to be established from the statutory statements (data-room item), so no release figure is asserted here. Sale is a legitimate outcome — the 2018 sale of the New York renewal rights to Plymouth Rock shows clean exits execute; passive continuation is the one indefensible option. *Deliverable: board recommendation by Q2 2027.*

**Net effect versus drift** (developed fully in the Financial Business Case): ~$260–370M of identified annual growth, plus ~$60–90M of defended premium (the AAA retention delta and bundle-retained auto), plus ~$250–350M of drift losses avoided — a ~$450–650M swing against the drift baseline by 2030–31, the components partially overlapping and reconciled line by line in the Financial Business Case — on $85–135M invested, with the combined ratio held ≤96.5% in the build years and ≤96% from 2028.

---

### What MAPFRE will NOT do

These are standing constraints, adopted by board resolution alongside the strategy, so that killed initiatives cannot creep back in through workstreams.

1. **No New England state expansion this cycle.** No New Hampshire, Maine, or Vermont builds; no Connecticut fix-then-grow. The entire NH prize at a heroic 4% share is ~$49M [2] — two-thirds of one Massachusetts share point winnable at marginal cost — fought against Safety and Plymouth Rock on shelves they own, by a carrier whose February 2020 "we want more exits" record every recruited agent can discover, with guaranteed profit-sharing floors as an unpriced contingent liability. **Revisit only after the YE2028 trajectory gates (auto ≥18.6% and rising, home ≥11.7% with attach ≥40%, CR ≤96%) are met.**
2. **No funded bolt-on acquisition.** The $50–300M single-state deal is demoted to an unfunded opportunistic screen with pre-agreed criteria: it has no identified target, is priced off a distressed 2015 comp no 2026 seller will honor, is silent on ~$100M+ of supporting surplus (true ask $150–250M), runs 2–3x Madrid's demonstrated ~$95M bolt-on appetite, and faces Plymouth Rock as the natural rival bidder. Buying premium is not growth and is never booked in the portfolio arithmetic. If a distressed renewal-rights book lands at Fireman's-Fund pricing, take the call.
3. **No equity checks to affinity partners.** MAPFRE may pitch AAA Northeast and other Northeast clubs a *non-equity* paid second-carrier or marketing slot as settlement architecture — but writes no equity capital and posts no contingent capital to any club. The live precedent is adverse: the AAA Washington JV (ACMIC) required a Q4 2025 capital contribution and spent months under AM Best review with negative implications before its December 2025 affirmation [52]. Conceding equity is the tax currently bleeding MAPFRE, not a strategy.
4. **No inorganic BOP/CMP acceleration.** The $20–40M MGA/renewal-rights spend is killed. Small commercial is a scale and STP arms race — carriers above $2.5B took twelve share points in six years, and Safety needed two decades to reach a $32M BOP book [37]. Commercial-auto defense and the CAR seat survive; BOP is gated behind the 90-day diagnosis and a shipped shared STP platform.
5. **No coastal homeowners scaling before an actual MPIUA rate filing exists — and never above the $30–40M DWP cap.** Bundled-only, inspection-driven, percentage wind deductibles [23].
6. **No direct-channel scale case in the plan.** The pilot and the 15–25K-policy steady state are funded; the 40–55K-policy / $70–100M year-5 forecast is killed. Measured CAC (<$500–900 [40]) and channel loss ratio authorize each scaling tranche — eight consecutive qualifying quarters before any scaled paid-digital budget.
7. **No un-gated commission escalation** against Safety's loyalty architecture. Only the loss-ratio-gated version ships; an un-gated arms race is matched within one cycle at symmetric cost.
8. **No umbrella as a standalone line** (~200% industry combined ratio in 2024 [25]) — bundle cement over MAPFRE auto+home only, conservatively underwritten at corrected rates.
9. **The unanimous negatives, permanent:** no NY/NJ re-entry (NY ran a −17.2% private-passenger underwriting margin in 2023 [55], and the renewal rights were sold to Plymouth Rock in 2018); no greenfield direct brand or Verti relaunch — Verti USA is MAPFRE's own controlled experiment in that failure, dead within four years with no disclosed premium [56]; no channel-differentiated web rate on the Commerce entity; no standalone workers' compensation on own paper (−14.6% ordered in 2024, +7.1% denied in 2025 — partner or front only); no scaled aggregator buying at the 2026 price peak; no in-house telematics stack; no core replatforming — the core is modern [51], and any "modernization" proposal is re-scoped as productization or rejected; no further MAIP credit-system advocacy [35].
10. **No West Coast growth capital and no drift:** hold/fix/sell review only, board recommendation Q2 2027.

## 7. Financial Business Case

**The strategy is worth a ~$450–650M swing in annual premium versus drift by 2030–31, purchased with $85–135M of investment over three years, roughly half of which is recovered inside the program by claims-economics self-help — while the combined ratio is held at ≤96.5% through the two build years and ≤96% from 2028.** This chapter makes that claim auditable: it states every assumption, builds the drift and strategy trajectories year by year, reconciles them to the initiative arithmetic in the pillar chapters, and shows payback on a cash basis a CFO can re-run. Figures are labeled **[F]** where anchored to the engagement fact base (cited), **[A]** where they are planning assumptions, and **[D]** where derived arithmetically from the two.

### The model is deliberately conservative: no rate-inflation credit, no double-counting, killed initiatives at zero

Four modeling disciplines govern everything below. A skeptical reader should check them first, because they are where strategy cases usually cheat.

1. **Every impact figure is a share- or mix-driven increment — no rate-inflation credit is booked.** MA private passenger auto ($7.381B DWP, 2024 [2]) will likely compound at ~2–3%/yr on rate [A], and MA home ($3.76B, 2024 [3]) at 5–7%/yr [A]. The model holds the auto market flat at ~$7.4B, allows home only well-below-trend growth to ~$4.3B by 2030, and values each impact line on one stated basis: auto lines at the flat $7.4B market; the home line as the *incremental share only* valued at the ~$4.3B conservative 2030 market (growth on the base share is excluded); commercial as share recovery valued at the 2024 market. Market growth on the retained book (~$180–260M by 2030–31 [D]) is symmetric upside to both cases and is excluded rather than argued over.
2. **Defended premium is never counted as growth.** The AAA retention delta ($40–70M/yr [D]), the bundle-retention auto spillover (~$20M/yr), and consolidation-protected premium (~$150M) keep existing dollars on the books; they appear on their own lines, carry no growth multiple, and the consolidation line is excluded from the swing total because it overlaps the share-recapture arithmetic.
3. **Initiative impacts are risk-adjusted midpoints, not stretch cases.** The judged-out numbers stay out: the year-5 direct-channel scale case ($70–100M) is killed; bind-online is booked at pilot-plus-steady-state only ($25–40M). The coastal home tranche is capped at $30–40M DWP and gated on an MPIUA filing that does not yet exist — it is booked inside the home line, not on top of it.
4. **Gated spend shrinks with gated revenue.** Urban scale-up, bind-online scaling, coastal, and the comp redesign release funding only on metric triggers, so the downside case (below) sheds cost alongside premium.

### The 2025 baseline: a repaired-margin, shrinking-share franchise

| Baseline metric (as stated basis) | Value | Basis |
|---|---|---|
| US group direct written premium (2024–25) | ~$2.6B | **[F]** [5][4] |
| MA private passenger auto DWP / premium share (2024) | $1,325.9M / 17.96%, #1 | **[F]** [2] |
| MA auto exposures / exposure share (Dec 2025) | 908,833 / 18.33% of 4.958M pool (22.1% in 2020) | **[F]** [1] |
| MA homeowners DWP / share (2024) | $399.1M / 10.62%, #1; 30.0% loss ratio; voluntary share 12.5%→11.0% (2023→24) | **[F]** [3][7] |
| MA auto market / one share point | $7.381B / ~$74M | **[F]** [2] |
| North America combined ratio 2023→2024→2025 | 105.4% → 98.7% → 95.4% (auto 97.8% in 2025) | **[F]** [4][12] |
| Commerce statutory surplus (YE2023) / dividends to parent 2019–23 | $561.0M / $246.7M | **[F]** [5] |
| AAA Northeast-sourced premium (2025); JMA expiry | >$200M; 12/31/2026 | **[F]** [6] |
| Average MA auto premium per exposure | ~$1,450 | **[A]** (derived from [1][2], mixed years) |
| MA exposure pool trajectory | 4.96M, −0.2% to +0.5%/yr | **[A]** [1] |

Two structural facts frame everything: the margin repair is complete (95.4% CR, record €139M North America result [4]) but was bought with ~4 points of MA auto share; and >$200M of premium — roughly 2.0–2.3 points of MA auto share [D] — sits on a distribution agreement that dies on December 31, 2026 [6]. The share-point conversion assumes **~75–85% of the AAA-sourced book is MA personal auto [A]** (the JMA covers AAA's Massachusetts territory, but the book includes home and umbrella; the exact composition is the first output of data-room request #1 — up to ~2.7 points if the book were entirely MA auto). The baseline is not stable. There is no "hold current numbers" option, which is why the honest comparison is strategy versus drift, not strategy versus today.

### Drift baseline: doing nothing costs $250–350M of premium and 2–3 points of share by 2030–31

The drift case assumes management executes competently on today's model but launches nothing: no retention offensive, no rater/API build, no bundle program, no telematics refiling.

**Drift assumptions [A]:** (i) the AAA book runs off at passive retention of 25–30% as MCIC absorbs January-2027+ renewals — a loss of ~$140–150M by 2029; (ii) underlying share erosion resumes at ~0.5 pt/yr from 2027 as Safety, Plymouth Rock and Arbella complete their repricing [11] and Progressive keeps compounding — ~1.0–1.4 pts cumulative net of overlap with the AAA loss; (iii) home share continues the 12.5%→11.0% slide [7] toward ~9.5–10%; (iv) the expense base (~29% of NEP [5]) deleverages against a shrinking book and the full MAIP overburden ($3–9M/yr drag [A]) persists, pushing the CR to 96–98%.

The drift share endpoint is auditable in one line **[D]**: 18.33% today, less ~1.4–1.7 pts of AAA passive runoff (the MA-auto portion of the $140–150M, on the ~75–85% composition assumption), less ~1.0–1.4 pts of resumed net erosion ≈ 15.2–15.9% — published conservatively (i.e., favorably to drift) as **~15.5–16%**.

| Drift trajectory | 2025A | 2026 | 2027 | 2028 | 2029 | 2030–31 | Basis |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| MA auto exposure share | 18.33% | 18.2% | 17.3% | 16.6% | 16.1% | ~15.5–16% | **[D]** from [A] above |
| MA auto PIF (exposures, K) | 909 | 900 | 855 | 820 | 795 | ~770–800 | **[D]** |
| MA home share (NAIC basis) | 10.6% | 10.5% | 10.3% | 10.1% | 9.9% | ~9.5–10% | **[A]** trend [7] |
| US group DWP ($B) | ~2.60 | 2.58 | 2.42 | 2.34 | 2.30 | ~2.25–2.35 | **[D]** |
| Combined ratio | 95.4% | 95.5–96% | 96–97% | 96.5–97.5% | 97–98% | 96–98% | **[A]** |

Interim-year values are interpolations **[D]**. Note what drift does to the concentration story Madrid worries about: MA auto *rises* to ~53–56% of a smaller US book — the drift case is simultaneously smaller, less profitable, and more concentrated by product.

### Strategy case: share walk to ≥19%, home to ~13%, DWP to ~$2.8–2.9B

The strategy case is built bottom-up from the pillar arithmetic, with each component phased over the years it can realistically land.

**MA auto exposure-share walk, 2025 → 2030–31:**

| Component | Share impact | Timing | Basis |
|---|---|---|---|
| Starting share (Dec 2025) | 18.33% | — | **[F]** [1] |
| AAA book net runoff (managed retention 50–60% vs. 100% today; ~75–85% MA-auto composition) | −0.9 to −1.2 pts | 2027–28 | **[A]** retention target and composition; book size **[F]** [6] |
| Underlying share erosion (drift assumes −1.0 to −1.4 pts) | 0.0 — **assumed halted by Pillar 1 [A]** | 2027–31 | **[A]** — see note below |
| Agency share recapture (P1: rater ubiquity, consolidator deals, repricing slippage at Safety/PR/Arbella) | +1.0 pt | 2027–29 | **[A]** planning estimate; tripwire = quarterly quote-stack metric |
| Safeco-disruption capture (~25K exposures ≈ 10% of Liberty's MA book) | +0.5 pt | 2026–28 | **[A]**; pool size **[F]** (Liberty ~4.98% MA share [10]) |
| Bundle retention lift (attach ~25%→40%; ~13,600 fewer defections/yr from 2028) | +0.4–0.5 pt | 2028–31, compounding | **[A]** attach; retention delta **[F]** [8] |
| Urban voluntary book (P3b, gated on pilot) | +0.2 pt | 2028–31 | **[A]**, pilot-gated |
| Bind-online steady state (15–25K policies, gated) | +0.3–0.4 pt | 2028–31 | **[A]**, CAC-gated |
| **Net (risk-adjusted)** | **≥19.0–19.5%** | 2030–31 | **[D]** |

**The erosion line is the plan's largest single unpriced assumption, and it should be read as such.** The walk assumes Pillar 1 does two jobs at once: it *halts* the ~1.0–1.4 pts of underlying erosion the drift case books from 2027, *and* it delivers +1.0 pt of recapture — a combined ~2.0–2.4 pt swing (~$150–180M of annual premium) resting on a $28–42M pillar. That is why the quarterly quote-stack metric is the program's first tripwire, why the pre-mortem's "parity without preference" scenario is treated as the second most likely failure mode, and why the downside case below stresses the erosion assumption directly rather than assuming it away.

The gross walk sums to ~19.5–19.9%; the headline target of ≥19.0–19.5% embeds a further haircut for execution slippage **[A]**. On PIF: at 19.0–19.5% of a 4.96–5.11M pool, MA auto exposures land at ~945K–1.0M **[D]**; the blueprint's ~975K–1.01M range sits in the upper half of that band because the urban harvest adds *new-to-insurance* customers — it grows the pool itself, not just MAPFRE's slice of it **[A]**.

**Home:** share path 10.6% → 10.7% (2026) → 11.1% → 11.7% → 12.4% → **~13.1% (2030–31)** **[A/D]**. The impact is the *incremental share only*, valued at the model's conservative ~$4.3B 2030 market (well below the 5–7%/yr trend): (13.1% − 10.62%) × ~$4.3B ≈ $107M, risk-adjusted to **+$95–115M** of annual premium versus today. Market growth on the base 10.62% share (~$55–70M) is excluded as symmetric upside, per discipline #1. The target is roughly 40% of the full auto-parity gap (~$275M [3][2] **[D]**), which is deliberately *not* booked this cycle and stands as named post-2030 upside. Every home dollar is written at a structurally better loss ratio than auto (30.0% MA home loss ratio in 2024 [3], a benign-weather year **[F]**, versus a 97.8% auto CR [4]), so the home line is CR-accretive even at normalized weather **[A]**.

**Annual trajectory, strategy case:**

| Strategy trajectory | 2025A | 2026 | 2027 | 2028 | 2029 | 2030–31 | Basis |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| MA auto exposure share | 18.33% | 18.5% | 18.3% | 18.7% | 19.1% | **≥19.0–19.5%** | **[D]** walk above |
| MA auto PIF (K) | 909 | 915 | 905 | 930 | 960 | **~975–1,010** | **[D]** |
| MA home share | 10.6% | 10.7% | 11.1% | 11.7% | 12.4% | **~13.1%** | **[A/D]** |
| Home + attach policies vs. 2025 | — | +5K | +18K | +38K | +55K | **+65–85K** | **[D]** |
| US group DWP ($B) | ~2.60 | 2.62 | 2.58 | 2.66 | 2.75 | **~2.8–2.9** | **[D]** |
| Combined ratio | 95.4% | 96–96.5% | 95.5–96.5% | 95–96% | 94.5–95.5% | **≤96% from 2028; ~94.5–95.5% exit** | **[A/D]** bridges below |
| MA auto % of US book | ~51% | ~51% | ~50% | ~49% | ~48% | **~47–49%** | **[D]** |
| MA all-lines % of US book | ~75–80% | ~77% | ~77% | ~78% | ~79% | **~79–81%** | **[D]** |

The 2027 dip is real and should be presented to Madrid as such: the AAA renewal wall hits hardest in 2027 even under managed retention, and the recapture engines are still ramping. The strategy case does not pretend the AAA book is saveable in full — it turns a ~$145M passive loss into a ~$85–95M managed loss **[D]** and back-fills with growth that compounds.

Note on the mix rows: MA auto falls from ~51% to ~47–49% of the US book — the plan diversifies by *product and customer* (home, commercial auto, urban segments) inside Massachusetts, not by state. MA all-lines premium stays at ~79–81% of the US book **[D]**, because every growth dollar is a Massachusetts dollar; the state axis is deliberately deferred to the post-2028 gate. Under drift, product concentration worsens on a smaller book; under strategy, it improves without a dollar of new-state capital.

### Reconciliation: the build-up ties to the pillar arithmetic line by line

| Source (annual premium by 2030–31, risk-adjusted) | Impact | Fact-anchored elements | Assumption elements |
|---|---|---|---|
| Agency share recapture (+1.0 pt) + Safeco capture (P1) | ~$74–110M growth | $74M/pt market math [2]; Safeco retirement & 22K-agency pool **[F]** | Capture rates **[A]** — tripwired by the quarterly quote-stack metric; erosion-halt assumption carried on the same pillar |
| AAA retention delta vs. passive runoff (P2a) | ~$40–70M defended | Book size >$200M [6]; MCIC's zero MA share through Feb 2026 **[F]** | 50–60% managed vs. 25–30% passive retention **[A]** (20–35 pts × >$200M **[D]**) |
| Group-deviation portfolio (P2b) | ~$18–36M growth | 280 approved groups; peers' 5–6% deviations; participation rule waived to 2030 **[F]** | 10–20K new group policies **[A]** |
| Home to ~13% (P2c) | ~$95–115M growth | #1 position, 10.62% share, 30.0% LR [3] | Attach lift 25%→40% **[A]**; incremental share valued at ~$4.3B conservative 2030 market **[A/D]** |
| Bundle retention spillover (P2c) | ~$20M/yr defended (retained auto) | 95% vs. 85% bundled retention [8] | Attach base is data-room request #2 **[A]** |
| Commercial auto share recovery + CAR seat (P2d) | ~$25–35M growth | #1 MA position (13.15% Feb 2026); CAR seat expiry 12/31/2026 **[F]** | Reversing the −4.5% leak and regaining ~1.5–2.5 pts of the $1.43B market **[A]**; market growth on the held book is excluded, per discipline #1, and RFP win assumed |
| Urban/credit-territory voluntary book (P3b) | ~$20–29M growth + $3–9M/yr drag shed | ~$2,900/car-year credit offset; 30.4% MAIP vs. 18.1% voluntary share **[F]** | 10K car years, pilot-gated; drag estimate **[A]**; the ~$29M of MAIP credit premium is a quota-share offset, not revenue, and is never booked as growth |
| Bind-online, agent-assigned (P3c) | ~$25–40M growth | Plymouth Rock Prime mechanic live since 2015; CAC benchmark $500–900 **[F]** | 15–25K steady state **[A]**, CAC-gated |
| Consolidation-exposed premium protected (P1b) | ~$150M defended | ~2.3%/yr agency ownership churn **[F]** | *Excluded from swing total — overlaps recapture lines* |
| Claims self-help (P4a) | $20–40M/yr ≈ 1–1.5 CR pts | 4.5%/6.2% industry recovery benchmarks, ~15% missed, $15–20B leakage [9]; $2.5B US paid-loss base **[A/F]** | +0.5–1.0 pt net recovery lift **[A]** |
| **Net vs. drift** | **~$450–650M swing: ~$260–370M growth + ~$60–90M defended + drift losses avoided (~$250–350M, partially overlapping)** | | |

Cross-check against the trajectory table: strategy DWP $2.8–2.9B minus drift $2.25–2.35B = $450–650M **[D]** — the two views tie exactly, with the partial-overlap treatment of defended premium noted above.

### Combined ratio: the guardrail, restated honestly, with both bridges shown

The CR guardrail is not decoration — it is the condition under which Madrid funds anything. Stated precisely: **≤96.5% in the two build years (2026–27), ≤96% from 2028, exiting at ~94.5–95.5%.** A flat "≤96% every year" claim would not survive the program's own year-1 cash profile — $52M of spend against ~$2.6B of premium is ~2 points if fully expensed — so the interim years are bridged explicitly rather than asserted.

**Interim-year bridge (2026–2028) [A/D]:**

| Component (pts on CR) | 2026 | 2027 | 2028 |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2025 actual base | 95.4 | 95.4 | 95.4 |
| Weather normalization (part of 2025 was benign-weather luck [4]) | +0.3–0.5 | +0.3–0.5 | +0.3–0.5 |
| Program spend, expensed portion (of $52M / $38M / $22M cash: ~50–65% expensed, the rest capitalized build — rater APIs, bind MVP, telematics platform) | +1.0–1.3 | +0.9–1.0 | +0.5–0.6 |
| Amortization of prior-year capitalized build | — | +0.2 | +0.3 |
| New-business strain (growth book seasons worse) | +0.1 | +0.2 | +0.3 |
| Claims self-help (ramp: $5M / $15M / $28M) | −0.2 | −0.6 | −1.0 |
| Mix (home/commercial growth at better loss ratios) | — | −0.1 | −0.2 |
| **Modeled CR** | **~96–96.5%** | **~95.5–96.5%** | **~95–96%** |

The underlying rate-earn assumption is stated, not hidden: earned rate covers loss trend in 2026–28 **[A]** — defensible while Commerce holds a 97.8% auto CR and competitors are still filing increases, and the assumption is symmetric with the drift case. If weather or severity breaks against the book, the two-consecutive-quarters-above-guardrail trigger pauses growth spend (see the governance section of the Implementation Roadmap & Operating Model) before Madrid asks.

**End-state bridge (to 2030–31):**

| CR bridge component (by 2030–31) | Impact (pts) | Basis |
|---|---|---|
| 2025 actual | 95.4% | **[F]** [4] |
| Program opex (P1–P3 run costs not capitalized) | +0.4 to +0.7 | **[A]** |
| New-business strain (growth book seasons worse than renewals) | +0.4 to +0.8 | **[A]** industry pattern |
| Claims-economics self-help (subrogation +0.5–1.0 pt recovery; touchless/steering +0.3–0.5 pt) | −1.0 to −1.5 | **[A]**, benchmarked to [9] |
| Home/commercial mix shift (30.0% LR home line grows faster than auto [3]; regional non-auto commercial CR 78.9%) | −0.3 to −0.6 | **[F]** loss ratios; mix pace **[A]** |
| Telematics enrolled-book effect (30% NB enrollment by 2028; symmetric structure only) | −0.5 to −1.0 (year 4+) | **[A]**; kill-trigger if approved discount-only |
| MAIP drag shed (urban credit harvest) | −0.1 to −0.3 | **[A]** [$3–9M/yr on ~$2.6B] |
| Fixed-cost leverage (growing NEP against ~29% expense base [5]) | −0.2 to −0.4 | **[D]** |
| **Strategy case 2030–31** | **~94.5–95.5%** | **[D]** |

The drift CR goes the other way for mechanical reasons: a book shrinking ~10–13% against a fixed expense base adds ~0.8–1.2 pts of expense-ratio strain alone **[D]**, plus the undiluted MAIP overburden and adverse retention mix — hence 96–98%. **The strategy case is the better underwriting outcome, not a margin sacrifice for growth.** The single biggest swing item — claims self-help — is also the least speculative: it requires recovering at industry-average rates on a $1.6B net-paid-loss base **[A]**, not out-executing anyone.

### Investment, self-funding, and payback: breakeven in year 4, ~$50–65M/yr run-rate return thereafter

**The ask: $85–135M over three years (~1–1.5% of annual premium), sequenced ~$45–60M / ~$25–45M / ~$15–30M.** The recommended funding mechanism matters as much as the amount: **a two-to-three-year reduced-dividend agreement, not a capital injection.** Commerce paid $246.7M of dividends to the parent through its loss-and-recovery years of 2019–23 [5]; retaining one-third to one-half of that run-rate — against a $561–684M surplus base [5] and a ~€118M annual U.S. result [4] — funds the program from earnings the U.S. unit already generates. That converts the Madrid conversation from a capital request (hard, precedent-setting) into an earnings-retention agreement (materially easier, reversible, and self-policing through the gate logic). One calibration note on the guardrail: the group's published 2026 target is a 93–94% combined ratio; the ~95–96% working bar used for the U.S. unit throughout this document is a planning assumption **[A]** — the U.S. book runs above the group bar on Massachusetts mix, and the funding paper should have Madrid ratify that differential explicitly rather than leave it implied.

One reconciliation the CFO should surface before Madrid does: the four pillar budget *ranges* sum to $97–160M at their extremes, wider than the $85–135M envelope. That is intentional, not sloppy — the gated initiatives (urban scale-up, bind-online scaling, coastal tranche, comp redesign) cannot all reach their maximum funding simultaneously because their release triggers are sequential and conditional. The envelope funds the pillar midpoints (~$112M at point estimates **[D]**) plus contingency; the gap between the envelope ceiling and the pillar-range ceiling is governance headroom that only metric triggers can unlock.

| Investment by pillar (3-year) | Range | Largest items |
|---|---|---|
| P1 — Agent desktop | $28–42M | Rater/APIs, STP, EZLynx, Safeco desk, consolidator agreements |
| P2 — Defend & densify | $38–61M | AAA retention ($10–20M), bundle tooling/comp, CAR RFP, group enrollment |
| P3 — Price & reach | $19–35M | MotionSmart refiling (<$10M), urban pilot ($10–18M), bind MVP ($5–15M) plus pilot media ($5–9M, partially offset by agent fees) |
| P4 — Claims & capital | $12–22M | Subrogation analytics unit, touchless scaling, operating-model change |
| **Program envelope** | **$85–135M** | Year 1 ~$45–60M |

**Illustrative cash view (midpoints, $M) [A/D]** — economic return = underwriting margin at a ~95.5% CR (~4.5 pts) plus ~2 pts of investment income on the float carried by incremental premium (new-money yields ~4% **[A]**), i.e., ~6.5% per incremental premium dollar; self-help counted at ramped run-rate:

| Year | Program spend | P4 self-help | Margin on incremental + defended premium | Net cash | Cumulative |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2026 | −52 | +5 | ~0 | −47 | −47 |
| 2027 | −38 | +15 | +5 | −18 | −65 |
| 2028 | −22 | +28 | +12 | +18 | −47 |
| 2029 | −8 | +32 | +23 | +47 | **≈0** |
| 2030–31 | −5/yr | +32/yr | +25/yr | +52/yr | +52 and compounding |

**Cumulative cash breakeven lands at year-end 2029 (year 4); the implied program IRR is ~35–45% [D, illustrative]; Pillar 4 standalone pays back in 12–24 months.** Peak cumulative exposure is ~$65M in 2027 — against $246.7M of dividends Commerce exported to the parent through its loss-and-recovery years 2019–23 [5], and a $561–684M surplus base [5] that the program never strains because there is no new-state capital, no M&A, and the CR holds inside the stated guardrail. At run-rate, Pillar 4's $20–40M/yr offsets ~50–70% of the annualized program cost — this is the "substantially self-funded" claim, stated precisely.

Three things this payback math does *not* count, all favorable: the terminal value of a defended #1 franchise versus a melting one (the drift case destroys ~$250–350M of annual premium whose replacement cost at direct-channel CAC of $500–900/policy [A] would exceed $150–250M); the option value of the bind-capable platform and telematics book for any post-2028 adjacency; and rate inflation on the larger retained book.

### Downside case: the program still clears its cost of capital when the big assumptions disappoint

Because the sizeable assumptions are independently gated, the honest stress test moves them together:

| Stress (all simultaneous) | Plan value | Downside value | Premium impact |
|---|---|---|---|
| AAA managed retention | 50–60% | 35% | −$30–50M defended |
| Underlying erosion halt (P1) | fully halted | only halved (−0.5 to −0.7 pt persists) | −$37–52M |
| Agency share recapture | +1.0 pt | +0.5 pt | −$37M |
| Home share 2030–31 | ~13.1% | 12.0% | −$45M |
| Urban program | scaled (10K) | pilot only | −$15M (and −$12M spend) |
| Bind-online | 15–25K steady state | pilot only | −$15M (and −$8M spend) |
| Telematics | symmetric approved | discount-only → killed | CR benefit −0.5 to −1.0 pt (and −$8M spend) |

Downside growth is still ~$120–220M of annual premium plus ~$30–40M defended **[D]**, on a gated spend that shrinks to ~$85–100M; cumulative breakeven slips roughly one year to 2030; the CR guardrail is unaffected because the kill triggers remove the spend with the revenue. **In no modeled scenario driven by the gated assumptions does the strategy underperform drift** — drift *is* the strategy case with every initiative set to zero, and drift destroys $250–350M of premium and 1–2 CR points. The true downside scenarios are execution failures — a channel-conflict backlash that puts the consolidation-exposed book in play (R3), or an AAA settlement that consumes cash without retention (R2) — which is precisely why the governance tripwires exist: the quote-stack metric (P1), monthly AAA-territory CAR tracking with the Q2 2027 re-scope gate (P2a), pilot loss ratios (P3b), and measured CAC (P3c).

### The YE2028 gates convert this model from forecast to contract

The financial case is falsifiable on three trajectory gates, reviewed at YE2028 and deliberately set at the strategy case's own modeled 2028 values — the test is "on the modeled path," not "ahead of it": **MA auto share ≥18.6% and rising for two consecutive quarters; MA home share ≥11.7% with bundle attach ≥40%; combined ratio ≤96%.** These are checkpoints en route to ≥19.0–19.5% auto and ~13% home by 2030–31, when the recapture, bundle, and gated initiatives reach run-rate. Hit them, and the diversification conversation reopens from strength, with a bind rail, a telematics book, and repaired claims economics already paid for. Miss them, and the gates force the re-scope before good money follows bad — the discipline Madrid did not have in 2017 and the reason this ask is credible now.
