## 8. Implementation Roadmap & Operating Model

The strategy's binding constraint is not capital, regulation, or competitive response — it is execution bandwidth. Fourteen initiatives will land on an organization that has shipped no growth-side or agent-facing capability on its modern Guidewire core in six years — no bind, no rater APIs, no digital enrollment, despite fielded customer-facing claims tools — and the four hard clocks documented in the Situation Assessment all expire inside the plan's first 18 months. The roadmap below is therefore built on three rules: **only three things are 2026 must-wins** (the AAA retention offensive, the CAR reappointment bid, and the Safeco transition desk — everything else may slip a quarter; those three may not); **every initiative carries one accountable owner, one falsifiable leading indicator, and a dated stage gate**; and **the kill list is enforced as governance**, so dead initiatives cannot re-enter through workstreams and fragment attention.

### Sequencing follows the dependency chain, not the size of the prize

The pillars are not parallel bets; each arms the next. Pillar 1 (agent desktop) is the delivery rail for Pillar 2's AAA re-anchoring and bundle offensive; the bundle is the retention armor that makes recaptured share durable; Pillar 3's telematics and urban plays run on the bind-capable rail and require agent goodwill that only visible Pillar 1 investment buys; Pillar 4's claims self-help funds the program's opex and repairs the experience flank that makes a passively held #1 share unstable. Three dependencies are non-negotiable:

| Dependency | Rule | Rationale |
|---|---|---|
| Bind-online (3c) launches only after Pillar 1 deliverables are visible to agents | Agent advisory council previews the design in the first 100 days; pilot go-live no earlier than H2 2027, after EZLynx/STP milestones ship | Activating direct bind before fixing ease-of-doing-business would inflame the channel exactly when the AAA dispute makes agent goodwill scarce; Plymouth Rock's agent-assigned model has run conflict-free in MA since October 2015 and is the proven template [1][2] |
| MotionSmart relaunch (3a) is gated on DOI approval of the symmetric structure | If the DOI approves discount-only, the program does not launch — that is MotionSmart 1.0 again (discount fell from "up to 10%" at 2021 launch to "up to 2%" today) [3] | Discount-only telematics donates premium on good risks; the DriveSavely precedent (10% at-quote, ±20% renewal swing) shows the symmetric filing is approvable |
| Coastal homeowners tranche (2c) is gated on an actual MPIUA rate filing and capped at $30–40M DWP | Pre-position underwriting, inspection and reinsurance now; write only after the filing exists (moratorium expired March 2026; none filed as of July 2026) | An immature coastal book plus one Northeast hurricane is a ratings event on a $561–684M surplus base [4] |

Second-order gates: the urban trilingual build (3b) scales from pilot (~3,000 voluntary car years in 2–3 credit-rich territories by mid-2028) to the 10K-car commitment only on pilot loss ratios and service metrics; BOP/CMP investment stays killed unless the 90-day commercial-auto diagnosis and a shipped STP platform reopen it; and each direct-channel scaling tranche requires measured CAC below the $500–900/bound-policy benchmark plus qualifying channel loss ratios for eight consecutive quarters [2].

### First 100 days (July 2 – October 10, 2026): three must-wins, one funding paper, no new headcount empires

The first-100-days plan concentrates on the perishable windows. The Safeco desk is the single most time-sensitive item on the board: Liberty retired the brand April 25, 2026, 22,000 agencies lost the only IA-native brand they had, and as of July 2026 no competitor has publicly launched a recruiting campaign [5] — a 90-day delay destroys the thesis because the realistic uncontested window is 6–12 months.

| By | Action | Owner | Deliverable |
|---|---|---|---|
| July 17 | Board resolution adopting the strategy, the kill list as standing constraints, and the YE2028 gates; CAR RFP war room stood up | CEO; President, Commercial Lines | Signed resolution; RFP workplan |
| July 17 | Safeco transition desk launched: fast-track appointments, Ivans Bookroll-equipped roll team, the no-direct-competition agent pledge (Strategy 1c) | Chief Distribution Officer | Public announcement within 30 days; appointments counted monthly to the CEO |
| July 31 | Interim funnel-leak fix live, ahead of any bind capability: every completed or abandoned website quote routed to a local agent as a consented warm lead within 24 hours (the 3c lead-recycling mechanic, launched immediately — zero channel conflict, value at any CAC) | Chief Digital Officer | Lead routing in production; monthly quote-to-bind conversion metric reported |
| July 17 | AAA retention task force stood up; litigation counsel instructed to open settlement architecture (renewal rights, member data, non-solicitation, paid second-carrier slot); AAA-sourced policy file pulled | Chief Marketing/Affinity Officer | Task force charter; data-room request #1 filled |
| Day 45 | Data-room sprint complete (bundle attach, group-deviation ladder, commercial-auto diagnostics, West Coast economics); 90-day commercial-auto leak diagnosis underway; EZLynx panel negotiation opened; first consolidator term sheets to Kaplansky/Alera and Duffy; MotionSmart symmetric filing build started; subrogation-analytics vendor selected; claims KPIs onto the executive scorecard effective Q4 | Named pillar owners | Fact base for all year-1 gates |
| Day 100 | Madrid funding paper delivered ($85–135M ask framed against $246.7M of 2019–23 dividends; year-1 tranche ~$45–60M); agent advisory council convened (enablement first, bind later, visibly); first group-deviation deepenings filed (top 10 anchor groups, 4%→5–6%); AAA re-anchoring campaign launched into the September–December renewal window; West Coast hold/fix/sell review and urban pilot territory selection commissioned | CEO/CFO; pillar owners | Day-100 board checkpoint: Safeco appointment count, AAA early reads, CAR RFP status, commercial-auto findings, filing readiness, Madrid tranche status |

The AAA campaign timing is unforgiving: MCIC showed no measurable Massachusetts share through February 2026 and no MA quote path on AAA's site, so migration concentrates in January 2027+ renewals [6][7] — every month of delay before the January 2027 renewal wall is unrecoverable retention.

### Year 1 (through Q3 2027): survive the two hard deadlines, ship the rails, prove the operating model can deliver

Year 1 is deliberately defense-weighted. Roughly $45–60M of the program (planning estimate within the $85–135M envelope) deploys here, concentrated in Pillar 1 engineering and the AAA/CAR defense.

- **Q4 2026:** AAA-book re-anchoring and bundle-attach offers running through the renewal-communications window; CAR commercial servicing RFP submitted; JMA expires 12/31/2026 — settlement negotiations continue in parallel, priced around the book (renewal rights, non-solicitation, member data), not the brand [7]. MotionSmart symmetric filing (8–10% at-quote, ±20% renewal) submitted; CMT contract extended; privacy-native consent architecture designed for either version of the pending MA Data Privacy Act.
- **Q1–Q2 2027:** the January renewal wall is the program's first live-fire test — monthly territory-level CAR share tracking against the 50–60% managed-retention target (passive runoff retains ~25–30%). Q2 2027 is the AAA re-scope gate: if MCIC prices irrationally with ACE-pool subsidy and takes the bottom quartile anyway, retention spend is re-triaged premium-quality-weighted, not escalated. West Coast hold/fix/sell recommendation to the board (Q2 2027); commercial-auto diagnosis verdict converts to either a disciplined-shrinkage confirmation or a share-loss repair plan.
- **By Q2–Q3 2027:** MAPFRE live on EZLynx's Massachusetts panel (it is absent today, while national consolidators standardize on that stack [8]); SinglePoint and Vertafore PL Rating connections hardened; STP new-business issuance for clean risks in production; five to eight consolidator platform agreements signed with change-of-control survival clauses. First 30–50 anchor-group deviation deepenings approved (routine filings; the 35% participation rule is waived through 2030 [9]). Bind-online MVP build starts only once the agent advisory council confirms Pillar 1 investments are visible in the field.

Year-1 exit criteria (all falsifiable): AAA-book retention ≥45% and tracking to the 50–60% band; CAR seat retained (or a priced exit plan if lost); ≥300 ex-Safeco agency appointments with first rolled books (10% pool capture ≈ 25K exposures is the plan-horizon target); MAPFRE rate present in ≥60% of top-400-agency new-business quote stacks; MotionSmart symmetric structure approved or the kill trigger exercised; claims KPIs live on the executive scorecard with baselines set.

### Years 2–3 (Q4 2027 – 2029): shift from defense to densification, open the gated offense

With the rails shipped and the two hard deadlines resolved, spend rotates toward growth (~$40–75M across years 2–3, planning estimate):

- **Bundle offensive at full tempo.** Agency-level attach targets with attach compensation, pre-filled home cross-sell at every auto new business, renters as the feeder. Target trajectory: bundle attach from an assumed ~25% baseline (data-room item) to 40% by 2029, home share from 10.62% toward ~11.7% by YE2028 — the stage-gate checkpoint on the modeled path — en route to ~13% by 2030–31 [10].
- **Loss-ratio-gated compensation redesign (1d)** ships in 2028, after platform agreements and rater ubiquity have already changed agent economics: growth and retention kickers pay only where agency-level loss ratios qualify. The un-gated version stays killed — it is the one move Safety matches within a single cycle at symmetric cost.
- **Telematics scale-up.** Enrollment pushed toward the 30% new-business benchmark (Allstate's 30–40% is the proven ceiling) via agent per-enrollment incentives and point-of-sale defaults; the third-party at-quote score filing (Arity IQ / TransUnion-CMT) follows the symmetric approval — no approved MA filing uses one today, and first-mover advantage is measured in the 12 months before Progressive matches.
- **Urban pilot readout — mid-2028 gate.** Target ~3,000 voluntary car years in the 2–3 richest credit-factor territories (offset economics run ~$2,900 of credit per voluntary urban car year, and Commerce currently absorbs ~1.7x its pro-rata MAIP share vs Safety's ~0.7x [11]). Pilot loss ratios and bilingual service metrics authorize — or refuse — the 10K-car commitment and the full trilingual claims build.
- **Bind-online pilot** launches H2 2027–2028 at existing filed rates, every policy agent-assigned for a one-time marketing fee with full commission and lifetime ownership; abandoned quotes recycled to agents as consented leads [1]. The 10K-policy pilot (~$17–20M premium, $5–9M spend, partially fee-offset) runs to measured CAC and channel loss ratio; the 15–25K steady state is the only funded scale case [2].
- **Claims engine at run-rate.** Subrogation unit and detection models mature toward +0.5–1.0 pt of net recovery (industry averages 4.5% of net claims paid, 6.2% among recovering firms, with ~15% of recoverable claims missed [12][13]); ePICS pushed toward touchless norms; texting and instant payment made default. Combined target: $20–40M/yr ≈ 1–1.5 CR points of self-help by 2029, which is what lets the CFO certify the program as substantially self-funded.
- **Coastal tranche** opens only if MPIUA files and wins a rate action — inspection-driven, bundled-only, percentage wind deductibles, hard-capped at $30–40M DWP.

**YE2028 is the program's master gate, set on the modeled trajectory: MA auto share (exposures) ≥18.6% and rising for two consecutive quarters; MA home share ≥11.7% with bundle attach ≥40%; combined ratio ≤96%.** These are checkpoints en route to ≥19% auto and ~13% home by 2030–31 — deliberately placed at the strategy case's own 2028 values, so the test is "on the modeled path," not "ahead of it." Passing all three earns the right to reopen the diversification conversation with Madrid; missing any one triggers re-scope, not escalation — and the 2017 retrenchment reflex is pre-empted by Madrid's explicit sign-off on this gate logic at program approval.

### Years 4–5 (2030–2031): compound, and convert the fortress into optionality

No new initiatives launch in years 4–5; the plan finishes what it started. Home share completes the climb to ~13.0% on a market growing to ~$4.3B; the enrolled telematics book matures into 0.5–1.0 portfolio CR points of loss-cost advantage; the group-deviation portfolio and urban franchise reach their 2030–31 run rates (+10–20K group policies; +10K voluntary urban car years if the 2028 gate passed); the direct channel operates at its measured steady state of 15–25K policies unless eight consecutive qualifying quarters have authorized more. The end-state book is diversified by product and customer rather than by state — MA auto falls from ~51% to ~47–49% of US premium even as it grows — and, if the gates held, MAPFRE enters 2031 with a bind-capable platform, repaired claims economics, and a telematics program that make any post-2030 adjacency (New England expansion, full home parity at ~$275M, opportunistic renewal-rights deals) dramatically cheaper than they would be today.

### Governance: a program office with teeth, an executive scorecard that prices experience, and a kill list that stays killed

**Program architecture.** A Growth Program Office (GPO) of 6–10 FTEs reports directly to the CEO — not to any pillar owner — with three functions: dependency and gate enforcement, a single program P&L reconciling spend against the Madrid tranches, and a monthly falsifiable-metrics pack. Every initiative has exactly one accountable executive:

| Pillar | Owner | Budget (3 yr) | Reports |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 — Agent desktop | Chief Distribution Officer | $28–42M | Quote-stack share, Safeco desk counts (monthly to CEO), platform agreements signed |
| 2 — Defend & densify | CM/Affinity Officer (2a–b); CPO Personal Lines (2c); President Commercial (2d) | $38–61M + priced discounts | AAA retention vs. wall, group-policy adds, bundle attach, CAR seat |
| 3 — Price & reach | Chief Actuary (3a); GM New Segments (3b); Chief Digital Officer (3c) | $19–35M | Filing status, enrollment %, pilot loss ratios, measured CAC |
| 4 — Self-fund & repair | Chief Claims Officer (4a–b); CFO (4c) | $12–22M | Net recovery pts, touchless %, cycle time, claimant NPS, West Coast review |

**Cadence.** Monthly: CEO reviews the three must-win metrics (Safeco appointments, AAA territory-level CAR share, CAR RFP status) plus the quote-stack tripwire. Quarterly: board reviews the full gate dashboard and one standing kill-list attestation — a one-page confirmation that no item on the standing-constraint register in The Recommended Strategy has re-entered under another name. Semi-annually: Madrid tranche review against the self-help offset schedule and the CR guardrail (≤96.5% in 2026–27, ≤96% from 2028).

**The operating-model fix is itself a governed deliverable, not an aspiration** — the rationale is argued in Strategy 4b [14]. What is new here is the mechanics: effective Q4 2026, claims-experience KPIs — touchless share, repair cycle time, texted-claims share, claimant NPS — sit on the same executive scorecard as the combined ratio, managed to cycle-time and NPS targets and never to headcount targets, with claims-leadership incentives rebalanced accordingly and the GPO auditing the scorecard conversion as a dated milestone [15].

**Channel-conflict governance.** The agent advisory council is standing, not ceremonial: it previews every consumer-facing digital release, and the sequencing covenant — enablement first, bind later, visibly — is board policy. The Citation/American Commerce paired-entity filing is held in reserve as the accepted MA template (Progressive has filed same-day paired entities since 2008) if channel separation is ever needed; no channel-differentiated web rate ever ships on the Commerce entity.

### Talent and technology: five hires that matter, productization only, no replatforming anywhere

The talent plan is short and specific; the plan fails without these roles filled by Q1 2027:

1. **Claims operating-model leader** — empowered by the scorecard change, owns cycle-time/NPS delivery; the highest-leverage single hire in the program.
2. **Settlement-savvy AAA negotiation lead** — the delta between passive runoff and managed retention is $40–70M/yr of defended premium on $10–20M of cost; this is a negotiation, not a lawsuit.
3. **API/digital product engineering pod** (in or near Webster, hireable or vendorable) — rater APIs, STP rules, bind MVP, group-enrollment flows, all as products on the live Guidewire core.
4. **Bilingual build for the urban pilot** — Spanish- and Portuguese-speaking adjusters, CSRs, and agency-development staff. This is the genuine talent lift in the plan and the reason 3b is pilot-gated and budgeted at $10–18M rather than the proposals' $8–15M; translation alone demonstrably fails with Spanish-dominant purchasers.
5. **GPO lead** — a delivery executive with carrier program-office experience, reporting to the CEO.

Technology policy is one sentence: **productization only, on the live core.** Guidewire InsuranceSuite has been in production for personal lines since 2020 [16]; every gap in this plan — rater ubiquity, STP issuance, bind-online, group digital enrollment, claims transparency — is a build-on-top, not a rebuild. Any workstream that arrives labeled "modernization" is re-scoped as productization or rejected. Vendor posture follows the same discipline: extend CMT (under contract, headquartered in MAPFRE's home market) rather than build telematics in-house; buy subrogation analytics; deploy Ivans Bookroll for the Safeco desk and consolidator rolls; scale ePICS and One Inc that are already licensed. The engineering priority is set by the agent-side facts established in the Situation and Customers chapters [17][18].

### KPIs and stage gates: what gets measured, when, and what happens if it misses

| Pillar | Leading indicator (cadence) | Stage gate & date | Miss protocol |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 — Agent desktop | Share of top-400-agency new-business quotes including a MAPFRE rate (quarterly); ex-Safeco appointments & rolled exposures (monthly) | EZLynx MA panel live by Q2 2027; ≥60% quote-stack presence by YE2027 | Quote-stack miss = early warning that enablement is achieving parity, not advantage → re-allocate toward group/bundle/urban moves Progressive cannot copy; never respond with un-gated commission escalation |
| 2a — AAA | Monthly CAR share in AAA-heavy territories; AAA-book retention (premium-quality-weighted) | ≥45% retention at the Jan-2027 wall; re-scope gate Q2 2027 (target band 50–60%) | If MCIC buys the book with ACE-pool subsidy, cede the underpriced bottom quartile, hold bundle-anchored accounts, redeploy spend to 2b/2c |
| 2b — Groups | New group policies; deviation ladder vs. peers (quarterly) | First deepenings approved 2026; 10–20K new policies by 2029 | Routine filings; low execution risk — misses indicate enrollment UX, fixed on the 3c rail |
| 2c — Home/bundle | Bundle attach rate (quarterly); home share (semi-annual) | Attach ≥40% and home ≥11.7% by YE2028 (en route to ~13% by 2030–31); coastal only post-MPIUA filing, ≤$30–40M DWP | Attach miss → attach-comp redesign before any coastal risk; cap and filing gate are non-negotiable |
| 2d — Commercial | CAR RFP outcome; commercial-auto book growth vs. market (quarterly) | Seat decision Q4 2026; diagnosis verdict day ~100 | Seat lost → priced exit plan; BOP stays killed absent diagnosis + shipped STP |
| 3a — Telematics | Filing disposition; new-business enrollment % (monthly post-launch) | Symmetric approval 2027; 30% enrollment by 2028 | Discount-only approval = built-in kill trigger: do not launch; pivot to the at-quote score filing as the second path to priced telemetry |
| 3b — Urban | Pilot voluntary car years; pilot loss ratio; bilingual service metrics (quarterly) | ~3,000 car years by mid-2028 with qualifying loss ratios | Gate fails → hold at pilot scale, keep Rule 29.F credit purchases as the bridge, shed no further spend |
| 3c — Direct | Measured CAC vs. $500–900; channel loss ratio (quarterly) | Pilot 10K policies; each scaling tranche needs 8 consecutive qualifying quarters | CAC miss → freeze paid acquisition, keep owned-traffic bind live (closing your own funnel leak is value at any CAC) |
| 4 — Claims | Net recovery pts; touchless %; cycle time; claimant NPS (monthly, executive scorecard) | +0.5 pt recovery by YE2027; $20–40M/yr run-rate by 2029 | Self-help shortfall directly reduces later-year discretionary tranches — the program's own funding discipline |
| Program | US combined ratio (quarterly); MA auto & home share | **YE2028: auto ≥18.6% and rising (two consecutive quarters), home ≥11.7% with attach ≥40%, CR ≤96%** | Any single miss → re-scope at constant budget; CR breach above the guardrail (96.5% in 2026–27, 96% from 2028) for two consecutive quarters → growth spend pauses before Madrid asks |

Two features of this table deserve the CEO's attention. First, every gate is falsifiable and dated — the plan can be *wrong in public*, which is what makes it credible in Webster and in Madrid. Second, the miss protocols are pre-committed and asymmetric by design: misses trigger re-allocation or freezes, never escalation. The historical failure mode of Massachusetts share wars — matching Safety's compensation or Progressive's media dollar-for-dollar — is structurally excluded, because the program only ever spends where the arithmetic of an uncopyable asset (280 group deviations, the home machine, the trilingual franchise, the agent pledge) does the compounding.

## 9. Risks, Dependencies & Mitigations

This strategy asks a fourteen-initiative program of an organization that has shipped no growth-side or agent-facing capability on its modern core in six years, funds it from a parent whose demonstrated reflex under stress is amputation, and times it against four hard deadlines — two of which (AAA and CAR) expire on the same day, December 31, 2026. The honest posture is not that these risks are manageable in general, but that each one has a named owner, a dated early-warning indicator, and a pre-committed response. This chapter registers the eleven material risks, states the hard dependencies the plan cannot function without, and closes with the pre-mortem: the three most likely ways this strategy actually fails.

One framing rule governs everything below: **the drift baseline is not risk-free.** Doing nothing carries a quantified cost — roughly $450–650M of premium swing by 2030–31, a book shrinking toward ~15.5–16% auto share, and a fixed-cost-strained 96–98% combined ratio (see the Financial Business Case). Every risk here must be weighed against that, not against an imaginary safe harbor.

### The risk register at a glance

Likelihood and impact are program-level judgments (assumption-flagged, not actuarial estimates). Impact is rated against the strategy-case arithmetic in the Financial Business Case; "Severe" means the program thesis itself is threatened.

| # | Risk | Likelihood | Impact | Primary owner | Decision gate |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| R1 | Madrid withdraws appetite mid-program | Medium | Severe | CEO / CFO | Year-1 tranche (Q4 2026); FY2026 results (Feb 2027) |
| R2 | AAA book loss exceeds plan; MCIC prices with pool subsidy | High | High | Chief Marketing/Affinity Officer | Q2 2027 re-scope gate |
| R3 | Channel conflict: agents read bind-online as betrayal | Medium | High | Chief Distribution Officer | Bind pilot go/no-go (post-Pillar-1 visibility) |
| R4 | Progressive out-executes on the agent desktop | High | Medium–High | Chief Distribution Officer | Quarterly quote-stack review |
| R5 | Safety/Plymouth Rock match the agent offensive | Medium–High | Medium | Chief Distribution Officer | Safeco-desk 6-month readout |
| R6 | DOI approves MotionSmart discount-only, or enrollment stalls | Medium | Medium–High | Chief Actuary | Filing disposition (kill trigger pre-committed) |
| R7 | Legislative loss-cost and privacy shocks (labor-rate floor, MDPA) | Medium | Medium | Chief Claims Officer / GC | 7/31/2026 session close; MDPA conference report |
| R8 | Northeast hurricane hits while the home book scales | Low–Medium (annual) | Severe | Chief Product Officer / CRO | MPIUA-filing gate; $30–40M coastal cap |
| R9 | Execution capacity: the program breaks at the executive layer | High | High | CEO (program office) | Day-100 checkpoint; quarterly milestone audit |
| R10 | Claims operating-model fix fails a third time | Medium | Medium–High | Chief Claims Officer | Q4 2026 scorecard conversion; 2027 KPI trend |
| R11 | Urban pilot underperforms on loss ratio or service cost | Medium | Low–Medium | GM New Segments | Mid-2028 pilot gate |

Three structural observations before the detail. First, the highest-likelihood risks (R2, R4, R9) are all already in motion — they are not scenario risks but base-case conditions the plan must beat. Second, the severe-impact risks (R1, R8) are both low-frequency, high-consequence, and both are managed by pre-commitment devices (gates, caps) rather than by monitoring alone. Third, no risk on this register is mitigated by adding initiatives; several are mitigated by refusing to add them — the "What MAPFRE will NOT do" list in The Recommended Strategy is itself the largest single mitigation in the plan.

### R1. Parent appetite: the 2017 retrenchment reflex fires mid-program

Madrid's mandate is real but conditional. The parent raised group targets twice — to ROE >13% and a 93–94% combined ratio for 2026 [1] — and its stated U.S. frame is "profitable growth" with "not planning to broaden our footprint" [2]. But the same parent exited five states in 2017–19, ran off Florida, shut Verti USA, and told investors in February 2020 it wanted further exits [3]. AM Best still rates the U.S. unit's standalone operating performance "marginal," with the A rating explicitly supported by the parent [4]. A bad U.S. quarter — one adverse weather half, one reserve strengthening — against a group printing 92.2% combined ratios invites the reflex.

- **Likelihood/Impact:** Medium / Severe — a mid-program funding stop strands the Pillar 1 build half-shipped, the worst of all worlds.
- **Early warning:** year-1 tranche (~$45–60M) approval slips past Q4 2026; Madrid commentary at FY2026 results (February 2027) omits U.S. growth language; the post-2026 strategic plan (first signals February 2027) reclassifies North America as harvest.
- **Mitigation:** the funding paper is built in Madrid's own grammar — $85–135M is one-third to one-half of the $246.7M of dividends Commerce exported through its loss-and-recovery years 2019–23 [5], structured as a reduced-dividend agreement (retained earnings, not new capital), ~50–70% offset at run-rate by Pillar 4 self-help, zero new-state capital, zero M&A, CR held inside the stated guardrail (≤96.5% in 2026–27, ≤96% from 2028) — a U.S. bar above the group's 93–94% target that the funding paper has Madrid ratify explicitly, given Massachusetts mix. Critically, Madrid signs the YE2028 gate logic *in advance*, so a single bad quarter triggers the pre-agreed gate review, not an improvised retrenchment. Quarterly reporting to the parent leads with the CR guardrail, not the growth number.

### R2. The AAA book walks faster and cheaper than the retention plan assumes

The JMA (>$200M of 2025 premium, ~2–2.3 points of MA auto share on the composition assumption stated in the Financial Business Case) almost certainly dies on schedule 12/31/2026: the injunction was denied February 3, 2026, and the court framed the whole dispute as money damages [6][7]. MCIC is backed by the Auto Club Enterprises pool (AM Best A+), filed sharply reduced MA rates effective 1/1/2026, and is building a 370-job operations center (a $7.1M private build-out, supported by ~$7M of Rhode Island tax credits) explicitly scoped for RI/CT/MA with NY/NJ expansion [8]. A pool-subsidized carrier can price below stand-alone economics for years. The tail risk is settlement failure *plus* irrational pricing *plus* AAA marketing the Emblem hard into the January 2027 renewal wall.

- **Likelihood/Impact:** High / High — passive runoff retains only ~25–30% (−$140–150M by 2029); the plan's 50–60% managed-retention target is the single largest defended-dollar item on the board.
- **Early warning:** monthly CAR share in AAA-heavy territories (MCIC showed zero measurable MA share through February 2026 [9] — any inflection is the signal); an MCIC quote path appearing on AAA's MA site; AAA-book retention through the January 2027 wall tracking below 45%.
- **Mitigation:** the bundle, not price, is the retention weapon — a home+auto+umbrella account is roughly 3x harder to move, and bundled retention runs 95% vs. 85% unbundled [10]. Premium-quality-weighted triage: if MCIC wants to buy the underpriced bottom quartile with pool subsidy, let it. Settlement architecture priced explicitly (renewal rights, member data, non-solicitation, paid second-carrier slot — non-equity only, per kill-list item 3 in The Recommended Strategy). Hard re-scope gate Q2 2027: if managed retention is tracking to passive levels, the $10–20M spend redirects to the group-deviation portfolio.

### R3. Channel conflict: the bind-online pilot reads as betrayal in a 100%-IA franchise

MAPFRE distributes through ~1,300 Massachusetts agencies that are nearly all also Progressive agencies. A mis-sequenced or mis-designed direct launch hands Safety — whose entire pitch is single-channel purity ("unlike some of our competitors who use multiple distribution channels" [11]) — a recruiting narrative, at the exact moment consolidators are industrializing book movement.

- **Likelihood/Impact:** Medium / High — a visible agent revolt puts the ~$150–160M of consolidation-exposed premium (P1b) in play and poisons Pillar 1.
- **Early warning:** agent advisory council sentiment at the design preview (day 45–100); MAPFRE's share of top-400-agency quote stacks falling in the two quarters after announcement; abnormal Ivans book-roll activity out of MAPFRE among top-100 agencies.
- **Mitigation:** the design is the mitigation — the Plymouth Rock Prime mechanic, running peacefully in Massachusetts since October 2015: every bound policy assigned to a local agent at full commission and lifetime ownership, rate parity across channels, abandoned quotes recycled to agents as consented leads [12]. Sequencing is non-negotiable: bind launches only after Pillar 1 investments are visible, endorsed by the agent council, paired with the no-direct-competition agent pledge Liberty and Progressive cannot make (Strategy 1c). The Citation/American Commerce paired-entity filing (the accepted MA template since 2008) stays in reserve if channel separation is ever needed [13]. Scale is capped by the standing-constraint register: pilot plus 15–25K steady state, nothing more without eight qualifying quarters.

### R4. Progressive out-executes anyway: enablement buys parity, not advantage

Progressive took ~7 points of MA share in five years, much of it through MAPFRE's own agents [9][14], and sets the table-stakes bar the Pillar 1 rater/STP build merely meets. The risk is that MAPFRE spends $28–42M to become adequate, share stabilizes for six quarters, and then resumes eroding as Progressive's segmentation and telematics depth (UBI as its single most predictive variable, at 3x the next best [15]) keep winning the quote stack.

- **Likelihood/Impact:** High / Medium–High — the +1.0-point recapture ($74M) is the plan's largest single growth line.
- **Early warning:** the quarterly quote-stack metric (share of top-400-agency new-business quotes including a MAPFRE rate) rising while bind rates stay flat — the signature of parity-without-preference; Progressive MA share resuming +0.5 pt/quarter growth after mid-2027.
- **Mitigation:** by design, the program's weight sits where Progressive cannot follow quickly: the 280-group §193R deviation franchise with the participation rule waived through 2030 [16] (open to Progressive by filing, but a 12–24 month group-by-group replication build against the incumbent's anchor relationships — see Strategy 2b for the explicit "Progressive files in 2027" stress test); the Massachusetts homeowners machine (#1 at 10.62% [17]) feeding a bundle Progressive has no in-state property scale to match; the trilingual urban franchise (3–5 year copy time); agency ownership-layer agreements. The tripwire response is re-allocation across these moats, not spend escalation on the contested desktop.

### R5. The regionals match the move: Safety and Plymouth Rock respond in kind

Safety runs the best agent-loyalty architecture in the state (loss-ratio incentive commissions, tiered clubs, #1-or-#2 position in 70%+ of its agencies [11]) and can match a comp escalation within one cycle at symmetric cost. Plymouth Rock grew its IA group 23% in 2024 [18] and owns the Prime template MAPFRE is borrowing. Either can stand up a Safeco transition desk; the plan's assumption is only that MAPFRE moves first.

- **Likelihood/Impact:** Medium–High / Medium — a matched arms race leaves everyone poorer; the Safeco capture ($35M) and part of the recapture arithmetic compress.
- **Early warning:** a public Safety or Plymouth Rock ex-Safeco recruiting campaign (none exists as of July 2026 [19]); competitor commission-schedule changes surfacing in agent channels; Safeco-desk appointment conversion falling below plan in months 4–6.
- **Mitigation:** speed on the perishable asset — the Safeco desk launches this quarter, with a monthly CEO metric, because the realistic uncontested window is 6–12 months; the consolidator platform agreements (P1b) sit at the *ownership* layer, where a change-of-control-surviving contract is a durable exclusivity no comp table matches; and the comp redesign ships only in its loss-ratio-gated form (kill-list item 7) — MAPFRE will not start the un-gated escalation Safety wins by matching.

### R6. The DOI approves MotionSmart discount-only — or approves symmetry and enrollment stalls

A discount-only telematics relaunch re-runs the documented MotionSmart 1.0 failure: donating premium to good risks with no surcharge offset (the discount decayed from "up to 10%" at the 2021 launch to "up to 2%" today [20]). The second-order risk is regulatory success and behavioral failure — approval of the 8–10% / ±20% structure followed by enrollment stalling in single digits, leaving Snapshot skimming MAPFRE's best risks.

- **Likelihood/Impact:** Medium / Medium–High — 0.5–1.0 portfolio CR points by year 4 (≈$12–25M/yr of loss-cost advantage) is the plan's principal pricing lever, and the only high-dimensional one MA law permits.
- **Early warning:** DOI disposition language on the filing; new-business enrollment tracking below ~15% at the 12-month mark against the 30% benchmark (Allstate's 30–40% proves achievability [15]); agent per-enrollment participation lagging in top agencies.
- **Mitigation:** the kill trigger is pre-committed in writing — discount-only approval means no launch, full stop. The filing leads with precedent (Plymouth Rock's DriveSavely holds 10% at quote with a −20%/+20% renewal swing; Snapshot operates with surcharges [13]). Enrollment is engineered, not hoped for: point-of-sale defaults, agent per-enrollment incentives, and the third-party at-quote score filing (Arity IQ / TransUnion–CMT) as the second path to priced telemetry that reaches the agent channel an app cannot.

### R7. Legislative shocks: the $55 labor-rate floor and the MA Data Privacy Act

Two live Beacon Hill processes touch the plan's economics. S.797 would mandate a ≥$55/hour body-labor floor against a market-weighted insurer-paid average of ~$49; on the Advisory Board's own AIB analysis, a $55 floor implies roughly +$22–36 of annual loss cost per vehicle, ~1.8–2.9% of average premium — industry-wide and rate-recoverable, but with a recognition lag, and with an annual ratchet built into the bill's permanent board [21][22]. The MDPA sits in conference; every plausible outcome makes telematics geolocation opt-in sensitive data, and the House version attaches a private right of action for large data holders — class-action exposure for any at-scale geolocation program [23].

- **Likelihood/Impact:** Medium (floor is a 2027–28 risk more than 2026; MDPA enactable through January 5, 2027) / Medium.
- **Early warning:** whether S.797 clears Senate Ways and Means before formal sessions end July 31, 2026 [22]; the MDPA conference report's treatment of the private right of action and the financial-institution exemption; First Circuit right-to-repair mediation status reports.
- **Mitigation:** build the +$6–9/hour severity scenario into 2027 rate indications now — the shock is symmetric across carriers, so the only real risk is filing lag; use MAPFRE's seat (one of three insurer seats) on the labor-rate Advisory Board to shape any floor toward survey-indexed designs [21]; reposition the DRP network as a cycle-time and quality lever, since a floor devalues rate negotiation. The telematics program is architected privacy-native from day one — opt-in, first-party, no third-party sharing, consent-logged — which converts the MDPA from threat to moat against incumbents carrying legacy data-broker plumbing.

### R8. Concentration and catastrophe: a Northeast hurricane while the home book is scaling

The strategy deliberately deepens Massachusetts. The two-axis truth: MA personal auto is ~51% of U.S. premium, but Massachusetts all-lines is ~75–80% — and the growth plan adds property exposure in a state where the FAIR Plan already insures ~40% of Cape & Islands homes and voluntary nonrenewals more than tripled 2022–24 (3,483 → 13,000+) [24]. The 2024 home book's 30.0% loss ratio was benign weather, not skill [17]. An immature coastal tranche plus one Northeast hurricane, on a statutory surplus base that troughed at $561M in 2023 [25], is a ratings conversation — and AM Best has repeatedly used geographic concentration as an explicit downgrade rationale, while MAPFRE's current rating is openly parent-supported [4].

- **Likelihood/Impact:** Low–Medium in any single year / Severe — a cat-driven CR breach triggers R1 simultaneously; this is the correlated tail.
- **Early warning:** coastal DWP approaching the cap; wind-zone TIV and PML drift in quarterly cat modeling; an MPIUA rate filing (none exists as of July 2, 2026 — the trigger is armed, not fired [24]); 1/1/2027 reinsurance pricing reversing the ~10–12% softening bought at 1/1/2026.
- **Mitigation:** pre-commitment, not prediction. The coastal tranche is hard-capped at $30–40M DWP, gated on an actual approved MPIUA rate action (the hearing process gives 12+ months of visible lead time), bundled-only, inspection-driven, with percentage wind deductibles (kill-list item 5). The core parity play is inland/suburban, where the $275M gap mostly sits. The group cat program plus softened reinsurance covers the tranche; the cap is a board-level constraint, not a management target.

### R9. Execution capacity: the program breaks at the executive layer first

This is the risk the judges rated highest and the one history supports. The organization fielded Guidewire (2020), ePICS, One Inc, and Shift — and still cannot bind a policy online, still sits in the bottom tier of J.D. Power satisfaction and claims rankings (see the Situation Assessment [26]), and has shipped no agent-facing API program. Fourteen initiatives now land on that same management bench, three of them with unmovable 2026 deadlines.

- **Likelihood/Impact:** High / High — bandwidth failure doesn't kill one initiative; it degrades all of them below the visibility threshold agents and Madrid require.
- **Early warning:** the day-100 checkpoint (October 10, 2026) missing more than one of its six readouts; EZLynx panel and STP engineering milestones slipping in consecutive quarters; key hires (API engineering, bilingual claims leadership, AAA settlement lead) open past 120 days.
- **Mitigation:** one accountable owner per initiative, named in The Recommended Strategy; a program office reporting to the CEO; a brutal 2026 priority rule — the AAA wall, the CAR RFP, and the Safeco desk are the only must-wins; everything else may slip a quarter, those three may not. The kill list is enforced as a standing board constraint precisely so killed initiatives cannot reabsorb attention. Sequencing rules (3c after Pillar 1; coastal after MPIUA; scale after gates) exist to keep the concurrent-initiative count inside the organization's demonstrated span.

### R10. The claims fix fails a third time — and takes the self-funding with it

Pillar 4 is the program's bank: $20–40M/yr (~1–1.5 CR points) of subrogation, touchless, and steering economics funds the opex story Madrid is being told. But the constraint has never been software — it is operating model, and this organization has already proven it can deploy tools without moving outcomes. Industry benchmarks say the money is real (salvage+subrogation averages 4.5% of net claims paid, 6.2% among firms with any recovery; ~15% of claims close with a missed subrogation opportunity; $15–20B of annual industry leakage [27][28]); capturing it is a management problem.

- **Likelihood/Impact:** Medium / Medium–High — if self-help underdelivers, the program's net cost to Madrid roughly doubles and the CR guardrail loses its cushion.
- **Early warning:** claims-experience KPIs (touchless %, cycle time, texted-claims share, claimant NPS) failing to appear on the executive scorecard by Q4 2026; net recovery rate flat two quarters after the subrogation unit stands up; 2027 J.D. Power claims score static.
- **Mitigation:** the operating-model change is the deliverable — claims KPIs on the same executive scorecard as the combined ratio, managed to cycle-time and NPS targets, never headcount targets; an empowered claims operating-model leader as a named critical hire; the subrogation vendor contracted with contingent, recovery-linked pricing so underperformance is the vendor's problem too. Repair cycle time is the industry's proven retention lever [29] — and claimants are the book's most shop-prone customers.

### R11. The urban pilot underperforms — loss ratios, service costs, or talent

The urban credit-harvest thesis is economically sound (each voluntary car year in the 11 credit territories earns ~$2,900 of MAIP credit offset plus new premium, and MAIP rates are the most adequate in years, with assignments down 38.4% post-July-2025 [30]) but operationally unproven inside MAPFRE, and the trilingual service build is the genuine talent lift in the plan.

- **Likelihood/Impact:** Medium / Low–Medium — deliberately gated; a failed pilot costs single-digit millions, not the program.
- **Early warning:** pilot loss ratios above the voluntary book after credit offset at the 12-month read; bilingual adjuster/CSR hiring below plan; service metrics (in-language NPS, cycle time) lagging the book.
- **Mitigation:** pilot-first in the 2–3 richest credit-factor territories (~3,000 voluntary car years by mid-2028); the 10K-car commitment and full trilingual claims build are gated on pilot loss ratios and service metrics; telematics-at-quote is the underwriting tool; budget already raised to $10–18M to price the talent lift honestly. A clean kill at the gate is an acceptable outcome — the credit-drag shed ($3–9M/yr) has independent value.

### Hard dependencies: what the plan cannot function without

| Dependency | Specific requirement | Consequence if missed | First test |
|---|---|---|---|
| Parent capital & mandate | $85–135M/3yrs, year-1 tranche ~$45–60M; Madrid sign-off on YE2028 gate logic | Program stalls half-built (R1) | Madrid funding paper, days 45–100 |
| Regulatory approvals | Symmetric MotionSmart structure; group-deviation deepenings; CAR commercial servicing RFP won; MPIUA filing before coastal scaling | Pricing lever lost (R6); $50M commercial line and seat economics lost; coastal stays shelved | MotionSmart filing 2026; CAR RFP decision by 12/31/2026 |
| Talent | Bilingual (Spanish/Portuguese) claims/CSR/agency staff; API and digital product engineering; claims operating-model leader; AAA settlement lead | R9, R10, R11 all worsen | Requisitions open day 15–45; 120-day fill tracking |
| Technology (productization only) | Rater APIs, STP rules, bind MVP, group enrollment on the live Guidewire core; CMT extension; subrogation vendor — no replatforming anywhere | Pillar 1 credibility with agents fails | EZLynx negotiation opened days 15–45 |
| Data (data-room) | AAA-sourced policy file; bundle attach on the 909K-exposure book; group deviation premium ladder; commercial-auto diagnostics; West Coast economics | Retention targeting, bundle arithmetic, and hold/fix/sell all run blind | Data-room sprint, days 15–45 |

### Pre-mortem: the three most likely ways this strategy fails

It is July 2029 and the strategy has failed. The three most probable autopsies, in order:

**1. Death by calendar (most likely).** The failure was fully visible in 2026. The Safeco desk launched in November instead of July — into a market where Safety had already announced its own program — and captured 8K exposures instead of 25K. The AAA re-anchoring campaign missed the September–December renewal-communications window, so the January 2027 wall arrived with passive-level retention; the Q2 2027 re-scope gate simply confirmed $140M was gone. The CAR RFP was treated as a procurement task instead of a board priority and the seat went to a hungrier bidder. None of these was a strategy error; all were sequencing failures on deadlines that were known, dated, and unforgiving. *The defense is already in the plan: the three 2026 must-wins are the only must-wins, with monthly CEO-level metrics — the pre-mortem exists so that no one can later claim the calendar was a surprise.*

**2. Parity without preference, and Madrid loses patience (second).** Pillar 1 shipped — competently, even. But rater presence and STP made MAPFRE *adequate* on the agent desktop, and adequate is not a reason to move a book. Progressive's segmentation stayed a cycle ahead; the quote-stack metric rose while bind share didn't; share stabilized at ~18% instead of recapturing to 19%. Meanwhile the program spent $90M, and at FY2028 results a Madrid facing its own 93–94% CR bar read "flat share, elevated spend" as failure and pulled the year-3 tranche — the 2017 reflex, on schedule. The recovery path was in the plan but not exercised aggressively enough: re-allocating from the contested desktop to the uncontested moats (groups, bundle, urban) at the first tripwire, and holding Madrid to the gate logic it pre-signed instead of relitigating the program quarterly. *Fail this way and the fortress thesis was right but the execution altitude was wrong — MAPFRE fought Progressive where Progressive is strongest.*

**3. The organization does what it has always done (third, and slowest).** Every initiative technically launched. But the claims scorecard change died in committee, so cycle times and J.D. Power scores never moved and the $20–40M of self-help came in at $8M; MotionSmart won its symmetric filing but enrollment stalled at 11% because agents were never paid to care; the bind pilot shipped six months late with a degraded funnel after two rounds of internal compromise. No single failure was fatal. Collectively they meant the program's self-funding evaporated, the customer-experience flank stayed open, and the inertia-held #1 share kept bleeding at ~0.4 points a year — the drift baseline wearing a strategy costume. *This is the failure mode the operating-model provisions (executive scorecard, named owners, kill-trigger discipline, gated pilots) exist to prevent; if those provisions are negotiated away in implementation, this chapter predicts the outcome.*

The common thread across all three: this strategy fails through softness in 2026, not through surprises in 2028. The board's risk-management job is therefore concentrated in the next six months — protect the three must-wins, enforce the gates and caps as written, and treat every request to renegotiate a kill-list item or a sequencing rule as what it is: the first early-warning indicator on this register.

## 10. Appendix: Methodology & Sources

This appendix documents how the strategy was built and how far each number in it can be trusted. It covers four things: (1) the four-stage adversarial process that produced the document; (2) the limits of that process — what is public-source fact, what is planning estimate, and what awaits the client data room; (3) the full fact-check register — 60 load-bearing claims independently re-verified, with the three corrections applied throughout the document; and (4) a consolidated, de-duplicated bibliography of every source cited across the 22-brief research corpus, grouped by type.

### The document was built to be attacked before it was built to persuade

The engagement deliberately separated fact-finding, verification, strategy formation, and synthesis into four stages with different owners, so that no single analyst's enthusiasm — or blind spot — could survive into the final recommendation unchallenged.

**Stage 1 — Multi-analyst research corpus (22 briefs, ~659 source citations).** Independent analysts produced 22 research briefs across five thematic clusters, each with its own sources section, as-of dates on every figure, and an explicit register of open questions not answerable from public sources. Analysts were assigned overlapping territory by design — for example, MAPFRE's financial baseline was built three times (parent-strategy view, company-profile view, statutory-statement view) — so that discrepancies would surface as conflicts to resolve rather than errors to discover later. Several were resolved this way, including the client's own "~80% Massachusetts personal auto" framing (corrected to ~75–80% of premium in Massachusetts across *all lines*, with MA private passenger auto ~51%), the group-level NAIC complaint-index distortion (pool subsidiaries inflate the group figure; the flagship Commerce entity indexes ~0.62), and a "$291M" group-premium typo in the state examination report (actually ~$2.91B).

| Cluster | Briefs | Coverage |
|---|---|---|
| Company | 3 | Parent strategy and mandate; US company profile; statutory financial baseline |
| Market | 7 | MA auto market; regulation; competitors; customer demographics; rate-filing price position; MAIP credit economics; 2026–27 regulatory horizon |
| Distribution | 3 | Independent-agent channel economics; agency consolidation and the Safeco window; AAA Northeast endgame |
| Growth options | 7 | Direct/digital economics; direct-to-agent calibration; adjacent products; geographic options; small commercial (two briefs); concentration-escape case studies |
| Enablers | 2 | Technology/claims/operations; telematics and UBI |

**Stage 2 — Adversarial fact-check (60 claims; 57 confirmed, 2 corrected, 1 unsupported).** A separate verification team extracted the 60 most load-bearing quantitative claims — the ones the strategy's arithmetic actually stands on — and re-verified each against primary documents wherever possible: statutory examination PDFs, CAR residual-market reports, SEC filings, DOI decisions, NAIC profitability tables, and the companies' own earnings releases, rather than the trade-press articles that first surfaced them. The test was adversarial: the checker's job was to break the claim, not to source it. Fifty-seven survived intact, frequently with independent second-source corroboration; two were corrected and one was ruled unsupported (all three detailed below), and the corrections were propagated through every chapter of this document. Where the fact-check flagged a nuance short of an error — e.g., MAPFRE's 97.8% "US auto" combined ratio is technically North America auto including Puerto Rico, or the +12.7% MA home-market growth is measured on premium paid rather than written premium — the document carries the precise framing.

**Stage 3 — Competing strategy panels with independent judging.** Three strategy teams built full proposals from the same corpus under different theses: **Fortress & Deepen** (win the home market and earn the right to expand), **Transform** (direct/digital channel transformation), and **Expand** (geographic and product adjacency). Three judges then scored the proposals independently on three dimensions — capital efficiency, executability, and competitive durability. Fortress & Deepen scored highest on all three panels (86 / 84 / 84) and became the spine of this document. The panel structure did real work: the judges killed Transform's year-5 direct-channel scale forecast and Expand's New England state builds and funded bolt-on acquisition outright, while forcing the winning proposal to absorb the losers' best content — the bind-online sequencing and guardrails and falsifiable-metrics governance from Transform; the CAR servicing-carrier deadline, the 90-day commercial-auto diagnosis, and the West Coast hold/fix/sell review from Expand. Where judges split, the resolution is recorded in the main document's "What MAPFRE will not do" constraints (e.g., affinity-club pitches survive only in non-equity form; the urban program is pilot-gated; the coastal tranche carries the stricter $30–40M cap).

**Stage 4 — Synthesis blueprint.** A senior-partner synthesis consolidated the winning proposal, the grafts, the judge rulings, and the fact-check corrections into a single decision-complete blueprint that governs every chapter. Two accounting disciplines from that blueprint apply throughout: *defended* premium is never counted as growth and never carries a growth multiple, and every impact figure is either traced to a cited source or explicitly labeled a planning assumption with a falsifiable leading indicator attached.

### What this process does and does not guarantee

**Public-source basis.** The fact base is built from public primary documents and reputable trade data. It is strong on market structure, competitor economics, and regulatory mechanics; it cannot see inside MAPFRE. Six internal datasets were flagged as first-priority data-room requests because the briefs could not source them publicly: the AAA-sourced policy file; the bundle attach rate on the ~909K-exposure auto book; group-level deviation premium; commercial-auto book diagnostics; West Coast block economics; and the 2024–25 statutory statements (paid NAIC/S&P/AM Best access).

**Known instrument limits, declared where they bite.** CAR market-share figures are written-exposure-based and cumulative year-to-date (comparable only same-month across years); NAIC premium shares differ modestly from CAR exposure shares; SERFF filing dispositions returned HTTP 403 to automated retrieval and require manual pulls; MAIP program loss ratios are not public (the $3–9M/yr assignment-drag estimate is assumption-flagged); consumer-aggregator premium figures conflict by methodology and are used only as context, never as planning inputs. Vendor-reported performance claims (e.g., Tractable's 90% touchless figure) are labeled as vendor-sourced. Each brief closes with its own open-questions register; none of those open items is silently filled with an estimate in this document.

**Negative findings are reported as negative findings.** Where the research needed an absence to be true — e.g., no competitor recruiting campaign aimed at ex-Safeco agents as of July 2, 2026; no MPIUA rate filing docketed; no approved MA filing using a third-party at-quote telematics score — the briefs record the systematic search performed and treat the result as "not publicly documented," not "not happening."

### Fact-check register: 60 claims, three findings against

**The three corrections — applied throughout this document:**

1. **J.D. Power New England auto satisfaction (corrected).** An early draft claimed MAPFRE scored 595 in the 2025 study and was lowest in the region. Verification against the archived 2025 rank chart shows MAPFRE scored **597** in the 2025 study and was **not** lowest — Progressive (582), Farmers (587), and The Hanover (594) scored lower; Amica's 735 stands. The 595 figure is MAPFRE's score in the **2026** study (released June 2026, where Farmers, at 567, was lowest). The document therefore describes MAPFRE's position as a chronic bottom-quintile pattern (597 in 2025; 595 in 2026; vs. Amica's 735), not as lowest-in-region.
2. **Subrogation benchmarks (corrected).** The "5.1% of paid losses recovered / 16% missed" pairing could not be verified against its original benchmark source. The document instead uses the peer-reviewed NAIC Journal of Insurance Regulation figures — salvage plus subrogation averaging **4.5%** of net claims paid across all firms and **6.2%** among firms with any recovery — and the consistently cited industry missed-opportunity rate of **~15%** of claims closed with a missed subrogation opportunity. The $15B+ annual industry-leakage figure was confirmed (estimates run $15–20B).
3. **MAPFRE group technology investment (unsupported — removed as a current fact).** The ">€600M/year in technology" figure is genuine MAPFRE messaging but traces to the **November 2018** MAPFRE Open Innovation announcement (€600M/yr technology + €100M/yr innovation); the October 2025 technology-hubs release cited for it contains no investment figure, and no 2024–25 primary source restates it. The document does not assert a current parent technology-spend number.

**The full register.** Sixty claims were tested; the table abridges each claim and its verification basis (primary source in parentheses; full citations in the bibliography below).

### Consolidated bibliography

The 22 corpus briefs cite ~659 numbered source entries; after de-duplication these resolve to **529 unique source documents**, consolidated below and grouped by type. Where the same document was cited through multiple URLs (e.g., a MAPFRE earnings release on both mapfre.com and mapfreinsurance.com, or an AM Best action via Business Wire and Morningstar reprints), one canonical entry is kept and mirrors noted. Series (CAR market-share reports, agency-M&A trackers, rate manuals) are grouped as series. Reliability tiering follows the order of the groups: regulatory/statutory records and audited filings anchor the arithmetic; company disclosures and rating-agency actions anchor competitor economics; trade and research sources provide market color; consumer-comparison sites are used directionally only.

| Source type | Approx. unique documents |
|---|---|
| A. Regulatory, statutory, and residual-market records | ~85 |
| B. SEC and securities filings | ~15 |
| C. MAPFRE group primary disclosures | ~35 |
| D. Competitor and counterparty primary disclosures | ~70 |
| E. Rating-agency actions (AM Best) | ~15 |
| F. Trade press | ~175 |
| G. General press | ~30 |
| H. Research, data, and benchmark providers | ~55 |
| I. Vendor and platform sources | ~40 |
| J. Consumer-comparison and review sites (directional only) | ~20 |

*Citation conventions used in this document: figures carry as-of dates; CAR figures are written-exposure-based, NAIC figures premium-based, and the basis is stated wherever the two could be confused; "defended" premium is reported separately from growth and never double-counted; every unverifiable figure is labeled an assumption and paired with the falsifiable leading indicator that would disprove it. Corpus compiled and verified as of July 2, 2026.*
